1001. ELIZA MITCHELL , feloniously killing and slaying her bastard child: to which she pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 21 .— Confined Two Years.
[trialAccount t18490409-1002]
1002. PETER LEITH , feloniously killing and slaying Solomon Abrams on the High seas.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD WILLIAM HAINES . I have been for several years in New Zealand, and came home as a seaman on board the ship Indian, of which the prisoner was chief mate—I joined her at Auckland; we sailed from there on 15th Oct—there was a Jew lad named Solomon Abrams employed on board the vessel in feeding the stock, which consisted of sheep, cows, goats, turkeys, fowls, and pigs—he continued to perform his duty till about 24th Nov., when he was not capable of doing so by weakness and illness—the vessel was then to the west of Cape Horn—I saw Abrams flogged the first time about a fortnight after we left Auckland—it was by the prisoner's orders, because he was dirty in the coalhole—he was flogged on the bare breach with a cat-o'-nine tails made of nine pieces of marline—he was lashed by his hands to the windlass, and had a dozen lashes—the first flogging did lot fetch the blood—he was flogged again about two days afterwards by the prisoner's order—that flogging drew the blood—he was about fifteen years old—he was in very good health when we left Auckland—I knew him on shore—I believe he was flogged the second time for the same reason as at first—the prisoner always flogged him himself—he had a dozen lashes on the second occasion—I do not know how often he was flogged afterwards—it night have been nearly every morning while we were on deck—I was in the chief mate's watch—I never knew him get more than two dozen—that continued until he took to his bed, about eight days before he died—he had no bed except what he brought on board—he slept in the galley, where the live stock were—I should not think he could keep himself clean there—the live stock caused more dirt than he could attend to—in the whole he wat flogged from eighteen to twenty times—I think he died between 1st and 5th Dec.—I never saw any one flog him but the prisoner—I saw the second mate strike him once—I have seen the prisoner strike him with the rope's-end—sometimes he was flogged at the windlass, sometimes over a bundle of hay, sometimes over the spars or over the guns; any place that was handy—his trowsers were always off, and he was stripped to his bare body when he was flogged—his hair was cut, by the chief mate's orders, close to his head—they said he had vermin in his hair, but I did not see any—I should think the vermin of the stock would have crawled to him where he slept—I saw him placed in a tub of cold water—we were then about 55 south latitude, and the weather was very cold—I should think there was about two feet six inches or three feet of water in the tub—the prisoner ordered him into the tub—he did not like to get in at first, and then the prisoner gave him a shove, and he ultimately went in—he had nothing but his shirt on then—as far as I can judge he was in the tub about an hour and a half—he was placed there to wash himself—he was not to say very filthy—he was dirty by knocking about the stock without shoes or stockings on—there was the manure of the animals on him—the prisoner used to come forward while he was in the tub and see if he was washing—he was washing his clothes—he was in a very weak state of health at that time—I did not see that his skin was broken—there was a very heavy fall of snow in the night, and in some parts of the poop it might have been six inches deep, and in some four—the boy shivered all the time he was in the water—after he had been flogged several
times he got full of sores, the same as any person flogged would—the sores never got well before he was flogged again, and that drew his bottom into a scab—I have often seen the blood come—the last flogging I saw him have was about eight days before he died—he got quite thin and weak—I first observed that about a fortnight before he died—I saw him flogged about three times after that—he slept on one side of the galley, the turkeys were on the other, right opposite him, and the sheep were under him—he could not help being dirty—there were no pigs in the galley; some were in the long-boat and some in the sty, which was between the long-boat and the galley, about two yards from where he slept—he had to clean the galley out every now and then to get the dung out—he had to tread in it when he got out of bed—the cook's galley was on deck exposed to the weather; if a heavy sea came over it must come into the galley—I never did see the sea come over—he slept in a place with a wooden covering and a partition from where the turkeys were—he slept on two boards put on the side, till he got a piece of plank put to keep him from falling out of bed—it was hard weather and cold while he slept there—that in my judgment was not a fit place for him or anybody to sleep in; I should not like to have slept there myself—I remember seeing him taken out one night, four or five days before he died, and placed on the fore-hatch—he was all over sores then, and was not sensible—I did not see any flogging after that—I went to see him after he took to his bed; I was not able to make him understand me—he often complained to me of his treatment before he took to his bed—he complained that he did not get his rations—the prisoner always behaved well to me—there was no surgeon or medical person on board—about three weeks before he died his bowels began to get loose, and they continued so till he died—he was often flogged for his bowels being loose—he slept in different places—he was a very good-looking, smart boy when he came on board; tall and thin for his age—after he was dead I observed a lump in his neck, about the size of an egg; I never saw it before—I never saw the captain present when he was flogged—there were about sixteen men on board the vessel.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know where this boy came from? A. Auckland—I do not know that he had been up the country—I never heard so from him—he was not a seaman—he lived with a person named Keasey, a general store-keeper—I knew him there about eight months—he had three places during that time—there might have been an inspector come on board when our ship came into the river; I did not see him—I went to the police-office the day after I landed—the day after I was paid by the captain; I received 2 l.6 s.—I did not receive my full amount; I ought to hare had about 3 l. more—the captain stopped 3 l., I do not know what for—I never heard the mate complain of my being idle or neglecting my work—the captain never told me the mate had complained—he might have said that on account of my sight being bad I was not able to do my duty as an able seaman; he never told me so—I embarked as an able seaman—I had been a mate before I embarked in this ship—Patterson had a defect in his eye—as far as I can judge, he did his duty as an able seaman—I do not know whether he was paid his wages, or what he received—he and I went to the Thames police-court to summons the master for our wages—I heard Pattersons summons was dismissed—I did not appear myself, because I was ill—I cannot swear whether we two were the only two who did not receive our wages—I was mate of the Sir John Franklin, of New Zealand—I did not run away from her, I was paid off—I got all my wages there—I was not charged with breaking into sume of the cargo—I did not take away any of the cargo—there
was a passenger on board the Sir John Franklin named Beattie—I have been in the navy, and left thirteen or fourteen years ago, because I thought I could do better—I did not get into any scrape there—I was never flogged—I have never been tried for anything—the last flogging Abrams received was eight or ten days before he died, and between that and the first flogging he had been flogged eighteen or twenty times—that might have been during a period of five weeks—that was before we rounded the Cape—he did not die till after we had passed the Falkland Islands—I swear I never saw the captain present when he was flogged, or heard him give any order for him to be flogged—the captain was very seldom on deck so early as four or six o'clock in the morning—it was generally then that the boy was flogged—they used to say that the boy was dirty, but I never saw it—I never heard the crew complain of it—the crew did not go up to the captain in a body and complain of his dirt, or I should have heard of it—when I saw his body I saw a few sores on his feet—they were scrubbed with sand, at the chief mate's orders, by the boy Herbert Taylor, an apprentice on board—they were small dry sores, just by the ankle, as if the skin had been knocked off, and they were healing—there were four or five of them, and I believe they were on both legs—I did not bear that he had got the venereal disease in the country—I have heard it talked about since the trial came on—I never heard of it on board—he never said anything about it, or said he was ill—if he had had the disease I think he would have come to me, knowing I had been so long in the country, and could judge of it—he never did come to me—there is a disease of that kind out there—Herbert Taylor had to rub his feet with a piece of canvass and sand, because his feet looked rather dirty—the first flogging was about a fortnight after we left Auckland, the next might be two days afterwards, and after that there was a flogging pretty nearly every other day—it generally took place between four and six, which was the chief mate's watch.
MATTHEW CAREY . I reside at 2, Queen's-court, Albert-street, Deptford. I was a seaman on board the Indian, and recollect Abrams coming on board at Auckland to feed the stock; he was then in a good slate of health to all appearance—there was a seaman, named Williams, on board, he complained to the prisoner that Abrams had vermin about his neck, and the prisoner ordered his hair to be cut—he made a mess on a sail, and the prisoner said he should not sleep in the lower forecastle any more, and he went down again and made a mess in the coalhole, and the prisoner flogged him for it; that was about three weeks after he came on board, and I believe it was the first time he was flogged—after that he was kept on the forecastle, and we were forbidden to go near him, because he had got vermin—his dress consisted of a cotton shirt and a pair of duck-trowsers—it was not very cold weather then, but we were getting into cold weather—he was kept there when his hair was cut, from about eleven o'clock in the morning till three or four in the afternoon—he was not subject to that treatment every day, only on that particular day—we went below about twelve the next morning and saw a mess on a sail, and he said he could not get the hatch off; the hatch was put over, but it was easy enough for any one to lift it off if he had placed his head against it; I have done it myself easily—he was in the habit of sleeping in the forecastle till the day his hair was cut; he was then put down in the lower forecastle—he used to sleep in the top-gallant forecastle; there were not enough berths for the hands on board; he never had a berth assigned him with the rest of the seamen—he used to sleep on the chest, and every time the door was opened, he was exposed to the wind and weather—we had some very severe weather when we got off Cape Horn, and it was very cold
there; we had very heavy seas—he then slept in the galley—the coppers had been taken out of the galley, and put-under the top-gallant forecastle—the galley was worse than the forecastle to sleep in; it was more exposed to the weather—he was placed there by the captain's orders—there was a top to it and sides; one door was nailed up, and the other left open—he could not get out away from the dirt—it was not nearly so comfortable as the forecastle—he had no hammock; he had a bed place, and two or three blankets—I know he had blankets, because I threw them overboard after he died, with his clothes—the mate gave me orders to do so, as they were in such a dirty state—I was in the mate's watch between four and six o'clock in the morning, that was when I saw Abrams flogged—I cannot say how often he was flogged; it might be more than a dozen times, or it might be less—I should not like to say he had not been flogged twenty times—I recollect his first flogging, that was for making a mess in the coalhole, about three weeks after he came on board—I do not recollect how many lashes he got then—I cannot tell how many days after that it was that he was flogged again; I think it was the next day; sometimes he used to get flogged every morning regularly—I suppose he was flogged eight, nine, or twelve times—his trowsers were pulled down, and he was flogged on the bottom, with a marline similar to this ( produced)—he was flogged for being dirty—it is my belief that he was frightened to come out on deck in the night-time—I never knew him dirty himself in the day-time; he said he could not help it—there was stock on board, which was placed where he slept—I remember his being told to go and scrub his clothes in a tub—I was not there at the time—I cannot say whether he was put into the tub or not—he came to me to lend him my brush; I lent it him, and he was left there to scrub his clothes—I saw him scrubbing his clothes, but I did not see him in the tub—he was all wet at the time; it was cold weather—after he died, I saw two large black or blue lumps on each side of his throat—I remember his being flogged for stealing two bullocks' or calves' tongues, from the galley—he had his rations served out like the rest of the crew, by Mr. Senior, the steward—he got them regularly if he was there; if not, people that were hungry eat them themselves—his share was always cut, and if he left it, anybody ate it who could get hold of it—the crew are not all on deck when the rations are served out; one man is generally sent aft to see them weighed.
Cross-examined. Q. Were they uncooked tongues? A. Dressedtongnes, hung in the galley for cooking—he ate them without being cooked—he stole them out of the galley, and so would I at that time if I could have got hold of them—we were short of provisions; we did not have near enough bread to eat with the meat, and we were frightened to eat meat, because we had not a full allowance of water—that began after we came round Cape Horn—I got my wages paid me—I stuck to my work; Patterson did too—I never knew him to lay up, except two days—the cook found out that Abrams was dirty; he first complained of it to the mate—after that the crew went and complained—they one and all said he should not sleep in the forecastle—I do not know that it was the crew that made him change, because if the captain ordered him to sleep in the forecastle we could not deny it—his place was changed by the captain's order, I cannot say that he was flogged by the captain's order—we did not complain to the captain that he was dirty, only to the prisoner—he was a very dirty boy—he dirtied himself almost every night—I never knew him to dirty himself till he was flogged—he was never flogged until he had dirtied over the sail—there was no occasion whatever for him to have done that—he was too lazy to get up—he was beaten so as to leave the marks—I
was never flogged—there might hare been five or six pieces of marline to the thing he was flogged with, and he was struck ten or twelve times—I saw the blood come—I cannot say when that was, but it was after two or three times—the tub was what we called the wash-tub, we all washed our clothes in it, and our faces and hands when we got up—we did not all get into it.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When this dirt was made on the sail how was the hatch? A. It was on, but not fastened—Abrams was not a regular sailor—the place where he slept was completely dark, you could not see your hand before you—Abraras said he knocked once or twice, but could not get the hatch open—I think if anybody had been there they would have heard it.
FREDERICK SAVILLE . I was a seaman on board the Indian. I recollect Abrams coming on board—he seemed to be in pretty good health then—seven or eight days after he came on board I saw the prisoner cut him across the back with the rope's end; he then had his clothes on—the first time he was regularly flogged was about three weeks after he came; that was across a truss of hay—I believe his hands were held and his clothes were down—the prisoner flogged him on the backside with a cat —I do not know how many strokes he had; they bruised him—I cannot tell what he was flogged for—from that time he used generally to be flogged every day—after he had been flogged be dirtied the place where he slept—the prisoner generally flogged him, sometimes with a rope's end, and I have seen him get a crack with a broomstick, or with a cat —I noticed that it had an effect upon his health about a week before he died—he then looked very bad indeed—I did not tee him flogged the last week—I remember his being put into a tub of water, it was in very cold weather indeed—the prisoner made him go in to wash his body—I do not know whether he was dirty—after he came out he had to wash his clothes too—I did not hear that he objected to go in—I did not see anything when he went into the tub—at the latter part of the time they said he was flogged for his dirtiness—I never heard the prisoner say anything about it—Abrams did not get food enough—I never heard any cause stated for hit being flogged except his dirtiness—he slept in the lower forecastle when he dirtied the sail—the batch was covered over, which would make it dark below—it was not battened down; he could easily get out.
Cross-examined. Q. You were not examined before the Magistrate? A. No; we were all on short allowance—we were all served alike—we had not enough bread or crater—there was enough meat—I do not know whether the mate was put on short allowance—we did not all complain of Abrams being dirty; a good many of us did—after he was dead I saw sores all over hit body—I did not see any on his ancles; I did not observe his legs—I only looked into the galley-door and saw them—I did not examine them—I cannot say whether the captain ordered the boy to be flogged or not—it is the rule of the ship for the mate to receive his orders from the captain—he was gene rally flogged when the captain was not on deck—I cannot tell whether it wat ever done when the captain was on deck.
JAMES PATTERSON . I was a sailor on board the Indian —I recollect Abrams coming on board at Auckland—he appeared to be in very good health then—I recollect his being flogged while the ship was on her voyage—I only saw him flogged twice with the cat—I should think the first flogging was about three weeks after we left Auckland—he was flogged by the chief-mate with the cat, for going into the lower forecastle and doing his business there—I do not know how many lashes he had—he was flogged again the next morning, or the morning after that—that was for a similar thing in the galley—the prisoner flogged him then with the cat —he was put over the end of the windlass and flogged—his hands were held—I do not exactly know
how many lashes he had; the cat was made of marline—on the last of these two occasions the blood flowed—he was in a very bad state, and after he was flogged, he was put in cold sea water, in a tub—I cannot say how long he was left there—he was put there to wash himself—he had a great number of floggings during the voyage—he was in a horrible state before he died; his feet were sore from going barefooted—he said his feet pained him—his drew was a very thin cotton shirt, and a thin pair of trousers—he was very weak and tick, and quite deranged five days before he died—he was not capable of helping himself—there was no surgeon on board; the captain used to give us medicine when we required it—I saw the captain and one of the passengers give him some medicine once, about a week or ten days before he died—he was more given to dirtiness at the latter part than he was at the commencement of the voyage; as his health got worse that habit increased—his bowels appeared weak, he said he could not unhook the galley.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you did not get your wages? A. Not all—I summoned the master, and the summons was dismissed; the mate was there, but he was not sworn; a charge was made against the mate then—that was before my summons came on; Haines made the charge first—I cannot say when that was exactly—I had no quarrel with the mate—he had not, to my knowledge, been the means of preventing me from having my full wages—the captain found fault because I did not steer the ship well.
JOHN BLKNNERHASSETT GODFREY , M.D. I am a surgeon at 3, Mount-place, London Hospital—I was surgeon on board Her Majesty's ship Diligence —I have seen the effects of flogging on the human system; diarrhoea and looseness of the bowels is one of the symptoms following upon it; if flogging is repeated from day to day fever is produced, and delirium generally attends fever—I have heard the evidence, and am of opinion that the treatment the boy was said to have received and the flogging were sufficient to undermine his health, and cause death—I see no other cause; the repetition of these floggings would weaken the stomach, and produce a shock to the system.
Cross-examined. Q. Your opinion is founded on the supposition that the boy was healthy when he went on board? A. Yes.
Witnesses for the Defence.
HERBERT TAYLOR . I was an apprentice on board the Indian —I remember Abrams coming on board at Auckland, and the first night when he came on board I noticed that he had sore ancles—he was turned out of the forecastle by the men because he was lousy, and he went to the cook's galley, where the carpenter put up a bunk for him; he only slept there one night. About ten days or a fortnight after he came on board, I noticed that he used to dirty himself—his clothes were dirty—he looked healthy when he came on board—I have seen him dirty many times, and Mr. Leith has ordered him to put clean clothes on; he would take them off again as soon as Mr. Leith was gone—I only saw him whipped once, that was about eight o'clock in the morning, with a piece of marline, across the bottom—if he had been whipped twenty times I should have seen it—I was in the mate's watch—I have been whipped with a rope's-end, but not in the same way—he was whipped with some marline—I was always in Mr. Leith's watch, but was not always on deck—I have heard the men say, when I have come on deck, "Abrams has been whipped again"—I heard of his being flogged three times—I saw him in the tub once, washing: Mr. Leith told him to get in, and he went in—that was when his hair was cut—his head was one mass of vermin, and Mr. Leith put some ointment to it—he was in the tub about a quarter of an hour, and then came out and washed his clothes—it was then comparatively
warm weather, and about three weeks after we left Auckland—I never saw all hands called up to see him whipped—I never sailed with Mr. Leith before—he behaved kindly to me, and I would sail with him again.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Are you going to sail with him again? A. I cannot say; the Indian has sailed—Abrams knew nothing about the duties of a ship—this Was my first voyage—I was whipped when I deserved it, with a rope's-end, but never with a marline—it blew very hard off the "Horn," and there was a little snow—it was not six inches deep on the poop, not more than an inch—I did not see the boy in the tub off the "Horn"—I never heard him scream out—I have heard him say he would not do it again, and that he could not help it—he used to wash in the same tab that the men did—he never used to wash himself at all without he was told to do so.
JOHN SHEDDON . I was carpenter on board the Indian —I remember Abrahams coming on board at Auckland—I noticed that his habits were dirty about a fortnight or three weeks afterwards, and he was put out of the forecastle on the complaint of the men—I made him a bunk in the top-gallant forecastle, it was protected from the weather—when he came on board he looked healthy and clean—after he was put into the galley I saw large black dry sores breaking out upon him—he was only in the galley two nights—he then went in among the stock for his own pleasure, no one forced him to go—he was a dirty, filthy boy—he then went into the lower forecastle, which was a comfortable plate to sleep in—there were sails there, and worn to hang up a hammock; I cannot say whether he had one—the burden of the vessel was 560 tons—half of the seamen had not beds, only bunks—the boy was better off in the lower forecastle than the men, and more clear of the weather—there was no hardship in placing him there—the first time I noticed these boils upon him was sixteen or seventeen days before his last illness—Mr. Leith paid every attention to him for eighteen or twenty days before he died—he was not whipped to my knowledge during that time—he would go to him, and order him clean things, he gave him two cotton shirts of his own—I never saw a cat-o'-ninetails on board—I saw the boy corrected three times for his filth and dirt, and that was all—I noticed a dry sore on the boy's finger on his left hand about three weeks before he died; the flesh was eaten right away—he has spoken to me about his habits at New Zealand, which might relate to that disease—the animals on board had no distase, nor any vermin that I know of—he slept on a rack that was made for the sheep, and was about four feet from the turkeys—he would never change his clothes except Mr. Leith would go and stand over him, and as soon as his back was turned he would take them off, and put on his dirty ones again—I have seen that more than once, twice, or thrice—his uncleanly habits lid not commence with his going to live with the stock—I should say, from the state of his head, he must have had lice months before he came on board—the skin was half off his head—he was turned out of the galley for being dirty in the saucepan in which the cook was cooking daily—his bowels were not loose at that time—I saw him in the tub for about five minutes, and every man was in it, more or less—I was in it—he did not stand in it for two hours, or an hour and a half, off the Horn—I never saw him in it but once, and that was for about five minutes—the weather was then quite mild.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When was that? A. About three or four weeks before he died, six weeks before we got to Cape Horn—I think he died before we got there—we had snow and sleet off the Horn once—it was a month or five weeks before he died that Mr. Leith gave him the two
shirts—he looked a clean, healthy boy before he came aboard—he began to get dirty a fortnight or three weeks after—I only saw Mr. Leith correct him three times—I never saw a cat-o'-ninetails on board that or any ship I was ever in.
MR. PARRY. Q. Have you ever sailed with Mr. Leith before? A. No—he was a humane, well-conducted seaman, I only wish every one was like him—I am not going with him again; the Indian has sailed.
THOMAS BEATTIE . I was an able seaman on board the Indian, from Auckland—I remember Abrams coming on board—about three weeks after we got to sea, I observed that his hands and feet were sore—he was labouring under a certain disease, known in New Zealand as the yhacyhac —I remember Mr. Leith ordering his hair to be cut, on account of the vermin—I have seen him whipped by Mr. Leith once or twice, and it was no more than he deserved—Mr. Leith's attention was called to his sores, and I never knew of his being whipped afterwards—he was very attentive to the boy—I have seen him order, and take him food and medicine, and give him clean linen, and stand by and see it changed—Robert Alexander called his attention to the boy's state four, five, or six weeks before his death—Mr. Leith was always liked by the crew—I had never sailed with him before.
COURT. Q. How did the boy seem for the first three weeks? A. He seemed healthy when he came on board, and did his work till we had some rough weather, ten or fifteen days after we sailed, and after that he was of no use
ROBERT ALEXANDER . I was an able seaman on board the Indian, from New Zealand—I remember Abrams being turned out of the forecastle on account of his dirtiness—I think that was better than three weeks after be came aboard—his hair was cut before that—I afterwards saw that he had on him a certain disease, which the natives of New Zealand call the yhacyhac —I saw boils on his hands and legs—I have seen the mate whip him, I cannot say how many times—the men were always growling about him to the mate he was very kind to the boy after I told him the state he was in.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How often used the boy to be whipped? A. I do not know: only when there was occasion for it—he was not flogged every time a complaint was made about his being dirty—as soon as the mate knew he was badly he knocked it off —I do not think he was flogged a dozen times altogether.
JOHN JOHNSON . I was a seaman on board the Indian —about three weeks after Abrahams had been on board, I saw he had some big dry sores on his hands and legs, and one on one finger—his feet swelled on account of his going without shoes—I have only sailed one voyage with Mr. Leith, be behaved kindly to me, and to the men generally.
COURT. Q. How often have you seen the boy flogged? A. Never—he looked clean and in good health when be came on board—he had a bed then—we turned him out of the forecastle because he was dirty—he never went aloft—he had been to sea before.
HENRY SENIOR . I was first steward on board the Indian, Abrams had not been on board above a week or ten days before he began to be lazy—I was present when his hair was cut on account of the vermin, his head was in a most filthy state, and also down his back and breast, he must have come on board in that state—he looked rather delicate when he came on board, but tidy and clean—about three weeks after he had been on board I noticed that he was full of sores about his legs and body, and one finger was completely
eaten to the bone—I never saw him whipped, and he was never out of my sight. THOMAS DOUGLAS. I was first steward on the homeward voyage. I remember Abrahams being turned out of the forecastle for being dirty—about three weeks after he had been on board I noticed that he had the disease upon him, it is there called the yhacyhac —I know what that is—I was in New Zealand four months. ( WILLIAM SMITH and HENRY SAGE, surgeons, gave their opinion that, from the evidence in the case, the deceased had died from the effects of disease; but also stated that continued flogging might have accelerated his death: the details of their evidence were not of a nature fit for publication.) GUILTY . Aged 21. —Strongly recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months.
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, April 17 th, 1849.
PRESENT —Mr. COMMON SERJEANT; and Mr. Ald. CARDEN.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant and the Sixth Jury.
[trialAccount t18490409-1003]
1003. GEORGE DAVIS, MARGARET M'CARTHY , and ANN WOOD , for a robbery upon John Byland, and stealing 1 watch, and other, articles, value 5 l.; and 3 shillings; his property; and, before the said robbery, beating and striking him: Davis having been before convicted.
MR. CAARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BYLAND . I am a journeyman dyer—I live in Long-alley, Shoreditch. Between one and two o'clock on Saturday morning, 24th Feb., I was coming towards home—I had been drinking but very little; I knew what I was about—I met the two female prisoners in Whitechapel, went with them to a public-house, and had something there; and while we were there Davis and another man came in—they knew the females, and they had something to drink—I remained there two or three minutes, and then left with the females—Davis and his companion stopped a little while behind—they then followed us, I saw them walking behind us—when we came to George-street Davis gave me a blow on the right side of the head, which knocked me down—when I fell, the two girls held my hands, and Davis got on my chest with oil knees—I had a watch in a small watch-pocket in my waistcoat, a guard round my neck, and a small metal watch-key, which I used to carry in my pocket with my money—I had 4 s. in money—while I was down, M'Carthy unbuttoned my waistcoat and coat, passed the watch-guard over my head, took my watch out of my pocket, and passed it to Wood—I called out, and struggled, and Davis said, "Let us murder the b—"—I got away, and met the policeman within two minutes—I told him What had happened, and returned with him to George-street—we saw M'Carthy against the door where it happened—I said, "That is one of the females"—we went up to her, and Davis was close by her—he said she was a respectable woman, and had been in his company all the evening—I told the policeman that I knew he was the man that knocked me down—I knew him by a scar on his cheek—he said he was not the man—I gave him in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Were you perfectly sober? A. I was; I had had but two pints of ale the whole day—I had been to see a friend who was ill over at Lambeth—I staid there till past twelve o'clock—I came over London-bridge—I was not wandering about the streets—I was coming to Whitechapel—I have been married; I am not now—I had gone
with the two females to treat them, and then they happened to be walking my way down George-street, into Commercial-street—I walked outside them, not between them—McCarthy was standing at the door of a house when I returned, and I said she was one of the women, then Davis came up, and I said that was the man—I told the policeman I should know him from a thousand, by a scar on his right cheek—I gave him in charge, and then I saw the scar—I did not know either of the prisoners before, yet I positively swear to them—Davis first said he had been with M'Carthy all the evening, and she was respectable; and then he said he had been asleep in a coffee-house till a quarter past two, and that he had been to buy some tobacco—he said I must be mistaken, for she was in his company a few minutes before—there was nothing found on him—I am sure Wood had the watch.
McCarthy. Q. Had I a bonnet and shawl on when I was at the door? A. No, you had not—I do not know the sign of the public-house we went into in Whitechapel
Wood. You know there was no one but you and I that went into the public-house; we came out, and in Thrawl-street you stood talking with me; the policeman came, and said to you, "You don't know what you are doing," and you walked away towards Whitechapel; I left you in Brick-lane. Witness. I took you and M'Carthy into the public-house, and I left it with you and her—I did not leave you in Brick-lane—Long-alley is down Commercial-street—one of Davis's statements was that he had been asleep in a coffee-house till past two o'clock.
WILLIAM KING ( policeman, A 416.) On Saturday morning, 24th Feb., I met the prosecutor at the corner of Wentworth-street, in consequence of what he said to me we went to George-street; we saw M'Carthy—he said, "This is one of the parties that has been robbing me"—I took her into custody, and while doing so, Davis came and said to the prosecutor, "You must be mistaken in this female, she has been in my company till within these few minutes"—the prosecutor then said, "This is one of the men who assisted in robbing me"—he had not before that given me any description of Davis but he then said there were two men, and one of the men had got a scar on his cheek, and after I got away to the light, I found Davis had got a scar on his cheek—the prosecutor had been drinking, but he knew what he was about; he talked reasonably.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw he had been drinking? A. Yes; I have been over to the other side of the water; I do not know what business a man has in Whitechapel to go from Lambeth to Long Alley—it may be a mile out of his way—M'Carthy was standing openly at a door—I had not seen her there before.
M'Carthy. Q. Was there not an officer who came and said I could not he the woman? A. He said he had seen you at your door; his name was Frost—he is now dismissed from the service.
SAMUEL GRBEN ( policeman, H 61). I took Wood into custody in a room which she occupies in Keate-street, Spitalfields—I found this common watch-key in a pail of dirty suds in the room—I took her between one and two o'clock in the morning, within half an hour or three-quarters of an hour of the robbery.
Wood. Q. You saw me with the prosecutor; were either of these prisoners with me then? A. No; the prosecutor called me by name, and I told him he had better go home—you went up Brick-lane with him to get some more drink, and in ten minutes, or a quarter of an hour, I heard of the robbery, and went after you—Davis said he had been to the coffee-house—I went there,
and found he had been there, and had gone out to buy some tobacco about a quarter-past one o'clock.
JOHN BYLAND re-examined. This is the key I lost—it is a miscast, and has a mark on it—I am certain of it; M'Carthy had a bonnet and shawl on when she was with me.
The prisoner Wood's statement before the Magistrate was read as follows: " The key the policeman found in my room has been there ever since I occupied the room; I was in the man's company, and drank with him in a public-house; there were only our two selves there; he treated the waiter to a glass of ale; he left with me, and was going home with me; he was going down the street, and then he said he would not go home, but if I liked to hare any more to drink I might."
M'Carthy's Defence. I was standing at my own door, between one and two o'clock, and the officer came and took me.
WILLIAM KINO re-examined. M'Carthy had no bonnet and shawl on at the door—it was not so light at the place where I took Davis that the prosecutor could see the scar in his face—there was not light enough to see it till I brought him to the gas lamp Witness for the Defence.
WILLIAM THOMPSON . I was at the Britannia coffee-house, and saw Davis come in, about five minutes past eleven, and about a quarter-past two, be went out to buy some tobacco; he did not come back again; he got into custody; he was not out before he went to get the tobacco.
cross-examined by MR. CAARTEEN. Q. What are you? A. A journeyman carpenter and joiner—I have worked for Mr. Burton, in Finsbury-square, for four months—I know the prisoner by seeing him in the coffee-house several times—there was another young man sitting in the next box to when I was; I cannot say who he was; there was only the waiter there that I know of the whole evening—the waiter has been here several days; he is not here to-day—I noticed the time when Davis came in, and it was a quarterp-ast two o'clock when he went out—the waiter came and awoke him up, and asked him for a pipe of tobacco—he said he had none, he would go and get some—I had no object in noticing the time—I do not notice the time when every one comes in.
COURT. Q. What was he doing in the coffee-house? A. He came in there and had a cup of coffee, and as soon as he had had it he fell asleep—I am not aware that the waiter said it was a quarter-past one o'clock when he left.
SAMUEL OREEN re-examined. I went to the waiter at the coffee-shop about half-past two—the waiter said he went out at a quarter-past one—the coffee-shop is about three hundred yards from George-street.
WILLIAM KINO re-examined. Not two minutes elapsed from the time of the robbery to my taking M'Carthy into custody.
DANIEL SUGGS ( police-sergeant, H 17). I produce a certificate of Davis's former conviction at Clerkenwell— read—"Convicted May, 1842, and confined six months, six weeks of the time solitary" —he is the man.
DAVIS — GUILTY . Aged 28.
M'CARTHY — GUILTY . Aged 20.
WOOD — GUILTY . Aged 24
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18490409-1004]
1004. JOHN HOLMES and RICHARD WILLIAMS , feloniously breaking and entering the warehouse of Henry Dymond, and stealing 237 pairs of boots and shoes, value 80 l.; his property.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY DYMOND . I am a vendor of boots and shoes—my warehouse is
at No. 2, Regent-street, City-road, in the parish of St. Luke's. At a quarter before six in the evening of 16th Feb., I left those premises safe—I was called up that night, went to my warehouse, and found the front door open, and two storeroom doors had been forced open—the bolt of the lock of the front door was turned back—it appeared to have been opened without violence—I missed upwards of 200 pairs of boots and shoes, worth from 80 l. to 100 l.—they were shown to me at the station by the police.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. What time did you leave the premises? A. My workmen leave at half-past five o'clock, and we leave shortly afterwards—I went out that evening with the foreman and another man; and afterwards finding I had left something behind, I went back, opened the door and got it—I then came out and locked the door—I know of no keys not these, which I keep myself.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Was this a dark night? A. I do not recollect.
WILLIAM CLARK . I lived in Baldwin's-place, City-road, but I have removed—I am a coal-porter. On Friday evening, 16th Feb., I was at the Wenlock-tap—the potman drew my attention to a horse and cart standing at the Wenlock Arms—that was about eleven o'clock—there was no one in the cart—I went and got a light to look at the name on the cart, as I thought it was lost—when I brought the light, a man ran up and said, "It is all right," and he got into the cart and drove off—to the best of my belief the man had a flannel jacket on, and a cap—he turned round, and went down the street—I went into the house again—the young man told me afterwards that the cart had come back—I went and saw that it had come back—there was a man in it driving—I could not say whether it was the same man that I had seen before—I followed the cart into the Wharf-road, and I stopped there talking to a man whom I met—I saw the cart again by Mr. Dymond's warehouse-door—they were putting something in it—I afterwards watched it as it drove down the City-road—I followed it to Bath-street, just opposite Willow-walk—I saw a policeman in Old-street, and told him—I could not say whether there were two, three, or four persons in the cart—I was behind the policeman, he stopped the cart—the persons in it immediately jumped out and ran away in different directions—I did not run after them, but stopped with the cart—I saw Holmes brought back by the policeman—I did not see the man in the flannel jacket sufficiently to know who he was—it was dark—I could not swear to the man—I have no belief as to who that person was—( looking at his deposition) this is my signature—I could not read this writing without I looked over it a good bit—this was not read over to me when I signed it—they asked me to put my name to it—I did not understand what it was for, nor what it was about—I knew it was about the hearing of these men, and then they asked me to put my name down, and I did—I said at Worship-street I thought Williams was the man in the flannel jacket and cap, but then he had a flannel jacket and cap put on—I said I thought he looked like the man, but I could not swear to him—I do not recollect saying he was the man before I saw him in the flannel jacket and cap—when they put the flannel jacket and cap on I said he was something like the man, I could not swear to him—to the best of my belief the person I did see had a flannel jacket and cap, but it was dark, I could not swear to it—it was a dirty flannel jacket—I cannot say whether the man in the flannel jacket and cap jumped out of the cart, I said I believed so—when I first saw the man in the flannel jacket had blown the light out—he came up before I had seen the name on the cart—whether the candle was out I cannot say.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How near were you to the cart when it was at the prosecutor's door? A. About fifty yards from it—it did not come towards me, it turned round—I cannot say how many persons were in the cart when it was at the door—I passed by it on the other side of the way.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLBSTON. Q. Was it not dark? A. It was—I was at some distance—when I was before the Magistrate I expressed some doubt about the man, and they put a flannel jacket and cap on him, that was while my evidence was being taken—I could not see whether there were two, three, or four men in the cart—it was a dark night, and the prosecutor's premises are in a very dark part of the street—I never saw Williams in my life before.
MR. PARRY. Q. Was the jacket put on while you were giving your evidence? A. I believe I had given my evidence before they put it on-then they put the jacket on to see whether I knew whether it was the man or not.
GEORGE GOODWIN . I am potman at the Wenlock tap—I know Clark—I remember on that Friday night calling his attention to a cart standing at the Wenlock tap—there was no one by it then—Clark went for a candle, and I went out with him, and at that time I saw a person standing by the side of the cart—he was very much like Williams, he was dressed in a flannel jacket and close cap—to the best of my belief it is him—I had not known him before.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Were you examined before the Magistrate? A. Yes—I did not see much of the man's features—they ordered Williams to put on the jacket and cap before me—I saw the man by myself first, and then I came out with Clark, with a candle—I cannot swear whether the candle was blown out or not—I had not a full view of the man's face—I saw him twice that evening; to the best of my belief he is the man.
MR. PARRY. Q. Did you see his side-face? A. I saw him sideways—from the observation I took of him, Williams resembles the man, but it was not a very light night.
JAMES MEAGER ( policeman, N 234). At a quarter past eleven o'clock, on Friday night, 16th Feb., I was on duty in Old-street—Clark pointed out a cart to me, twenty or thirty yards off; there were three men in it—I followed it, and called out to them to stop—the driver was dressed in a scull cap, and a white flannel jacket; I have no belief as to who he was—I got up to the horse's head, and seized the reins, and stopped the cart—the persons who were in it jumped out, and ran away—I cannot say whether the cap and jacket now produced are what the man had on; it was a cap and jacket of this description—the persons who jumped out ran in the same direction as far as Willow-street—two of them turned down there, and Holmes kept on a-head—I followed, and took him into custody—I never lost sight of him from the time he jumped out of the cart; I am confident he is one of the men—he was stopped by Baker—I told him he was one of the men that jumped out of the cart—he said he was not—he appeared as if he had been running; he was very much excited and agitated—Clark minded the cart and horse while I was running—I found in the cart sixty-six pairs of Wellington boots, and 170 pairs of women's boots, and two pairs of shoes, and three crowbars—the name of "Richard Williams, Turk-street, Bethnal-green," was on the cart; it was then about twenty minutes past eleven—when I first took them it was about a quarter-past eleven—I afterwards went with Ball and Smith to Turk-street, Bethnal-green, about one—Williams would have had abundance of time to get home, supposing he had been the
person who jumped out of the cart—he is a marine-store dealer—Ball and Smith searched the house—it is about three-quarters of a mile from where I found the cart—I took the articles in the cart to the station—the prosecutor identified them.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How far had Holmes run before you got him? A. About 200 yards; it might have been between 200 and 300 yards—I stopped the cart at the corner of Willow-walk—Holmes was apprehended within a few yards of Leonard-square—I was between twenty and thirty yards behind him—he had no turnings to take before I came to him; it was in a direct line—I swear he did not run down Willow-walk; the other two men did; he ran straight a-head down Paul-street; he was in Paul-street, at the corner of Leonard-square, when he was apprehended.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Had you seen Clark before you stopped the cart? A. Yes; I then went and got hold of the head of the horse; the men were driving pretty fast, between five and six miles an how—I do not suppose I was running by the side of the cart above half a minute; it was a starlight night, but a little hazy.
GEORGE BAKER ( policeman, G 228). On 16th Feb., about ten minutes past eleven o'clock, I was on duty in Leonard-square—I heard a cry of "Stop thief!" and saw Holmes running towards me—I stopped him—he asked me what I was stopping him for—I told him I would tell him presently—Meager, who was following him, came up, and said, "Bring him back," and we took him back into custody to the cart, which was standing at the end of Bath-street, opposite Willow-walk.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Could you see down Paul-street from the cart? A. I cannot say exactly whether I could or not—I was in Leonard-street, which runs across the square—I stopped Holmes in Paul-street.
WILLIAM EDWARD BALL ( police-sergeant, N 54). Between twelve and one o'clock on the night of 16th Feb., I had information of this robbery—I went with Smith and Meager to 6, Turk-street, Bethnal-green, where Williams lives—it is a coal-shed at the back, and a marine-store shop in front—there were three lights; one was burning in the shed, and another in the back-room—I tapped at the window, and heard a scuffling noise inside—Williams put his head out of the bed-room window and said, "What is it?"—I said, "Is that you, Williams? is the horse come home?"—he said, "No; have you been after anything?"—I was in plain clothes—I asked him to come down, and he came and opened the front door, and said, "There you be" —I told him we were two police-constables, and we should take him into custody for a robbery in the City-road—he was undressed—he had neither trowsers or drawers on, and I observed he was rather excited—I said I should take him into custody for being concerned with others in a robbery in the City-road—he said, "Robbery, I know nothing of any robbery; a man came and borrowed my horse and cart; I suspected something was wrong, and I sent my boy to watch him, and he came back and told me that he had lost him somewhere near Mile-end-road"—I asked him who the man was that hired his cart—he said, "I do not know him; he was a short man, and dressed in a light jacket"—he said he would do his best endeavours to identify him—I told him to dress himself—he did so, and we went to the station—he said the man wanted the cart to remove some goods out of Thomas-street—I had partly searched his house—I went back again and asked his wife for her husband's jacket, and she gave me this jacket now produced—this cap was given to Smith the next day—I found a coat, and a quantity of new metal, which is not here—I went again the next morning—I
found a piece of silk-velvet and some handkerchiefs in a cupboard in the back room.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Have you told us all that took place? A. Perhaps not—I have to the best of my recollection-"I think when he said he would do his best endeavours to identify the man, that he said he wished he could see him—I think those words were mentioned—his wife gave me this jacket from a cupboard in the front room—that was after he was in custody—when I took him he did not put the cap on to go out, and I did not tell him to put his hat on—I noticed a cap in the room; I forget where it was—he did not put it on—something took place in the room about one of the men wearing a cap and a pilot coat—I did not tell him when he was going away to put his hat on—I remember now his saying that he would put on his hat—this was in the back room, the bed-room—I saw his cap there—he went in this pilot coat and hat.
JOHN SMITH ( policeman, AT 121). I went with Baker to Williams's house on that evening—when he tapped at the window I heard a noise inside as of some persons moving about—Williams opened the window and said, "Who it there?"—Baker said, "Is that you, Williams: is the horse come home?" Williams said, "No"—Baker said, "Come down, I want to speak to you"—he came down and opened the front door—he had either trowsers or drawers on—he appeared flurried—Baker said he was taken into custody for a robbery in the City-road—he said, "Robbery, I know of no robbery"—Baker said, "For a robbery in Regent-street, City-road; your horse and cart is at the station"—he said, "I lent my horse and cart to a short dark man, dressed in a cap and light jacket"—Baker said, "Do you know who he is?"—he said, No; I should be very glad to identify him if you take him"—he said after the man was gone he had his misgivings, and sent the boy after him—I saw this flannel jacket found—this cap was given to me the next morning by Williams's wife.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Do you know anything of 5 l. reward? A. The first bill I saw was sticking outside the station—I do not recollect that Williams took up his cap to put on, and that Baker said, "Take your hat, and not your cap"—I believe the word "hat" was mentioned by Baker, but what I do not know.
HOLMES — GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Ten Years.
WILLIAMS — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18490409-1005]
1005. EDWARD STANNARD , unlawfully pledging and converting to his own use 15 securities, value 100 l.; the property of William Walker, which were entrusted to him for a special purpose.
MESSRS. BODKIN and HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WALKER . I am a clerk, living in Charlotte-street, Fitsroy-square. On 24th July I saw an advertisement in the Times newspaper, that a young man of respectability was wanted as clerk, to fill a mercantile situation, at a salary of 70 l. per annum, and that good security was required to the amount of 100 l.—In consequence of that, I sent a letter to the address stated, and got an answer, which I have lost—in consequence of that answer, I went to 3, Pancras-lane—I saw over the door, "Edward Stannard, winemerchant"—I saw the prisoner—I told him I had called respecting the advertisement, and my note was produced—he said he liked the appearance of the writing, and he thought I should suit him—he asked if I was a married man, as the salary was so small—I said, no, I was not, but I was in hopes it
might lead to something better eventually—I had stated in my letter that I had been engaged on the London and Brighton Railway, and gave him a reference to Mr. Buckland, the secretary—he said he would write to him, and he made an appointment for Thursday, 27th July—I called on that day—he said he had had a very pretty note from Mr. Buckland, and was perfectly satisfied—I had stated that, if it were necessary, I could lodge railway shares to the amount of 100 l.—the shares I had were of the Reading, Reigate, and Guildford Railway—on 27th July the prisoner said he knew nothing of railway shares—I told him the security was equal to money—(the shares were what they call guarantee stock)—he made an appointment for me on the following Monday, and I was to take my shares with me, but I forgot them, and I was to bring them on the following Monday—he gave me two or three cards, and said I was to present them to my friends—this is one of them—I went on the following Monday, and entered on my business—I am not certain, but I think I was sent down to the Docks to inquire if certain vessels had arrived—I think one of the ships did come in, but there was nothing in it for him—I did not see any wines in wood or bottles on his premises—I took my shares to him on the 9th of Aug.—I could have got 100 l. for them, or perhaps a pound or two more at the market price—they are negotiable by transfer—I gave them to the prisoner as security for integrity—he had no authority whatever to dispose of them, or to raise money on them—I continued as his clerk for three weeks—I never saw any wines bought or sold—on the 29th Aug. I went to the warehouse as usual, and I found a van at the door, with empty bottles and hampers—I went down into the cellar, and there was not the slightest appearance of any wine having been there—I stopped the van, and went to Mr. Exley's—on my return home I found this letter, by post, in the prisoner's writing—he did not call on me again—I went to Mr. Exley's office, and saw the prisoner there—he had given me an I O U about a week before, and he offered to meet me at Mr. Exley's—I never got my shares back—I had before ascertained from the prisoner that they were in the hands of Mr. Exley—the prisoner got me to sign a memorandum—he pressed me very much to allow him a week to settle the matter, and after a little consideration I consented—his wife was there, and she importuned me as well—that was on the Monday, I believe, and on the Wednesday I was to meet him at Mr. Exley's, and he was to arrange with Mr. Exley that the shares were to be given up to me—on the 9th Sept. I was to have 9 l., for salary given up to me, and on this consideration I said I would not take any proceedings against him—I never got my shares, nor the 9 l., salary—I do not know what became of the prisoner, I lost sight of him then.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. My friend put into your mouth that the prisoner got you to sign a document; how old are you? A. Thirty-six—I knew what I was signing, but it was only to be in existence for a week—I knew that I was promising to take no proceedings against him, and if I had received my money and shares I would not—I took proceedings against him about a month ago—I left nearly five months pass—I had not had any communication with him during that time—I met him in Dec. in Mr. Exley's office—I did not tell him that if he would give up the shares that would satisfy me—I had gone to Mr. Exley's that day for the purpose of coming to some arrangement with Mr. Exley—I said, if I possibly could, I would call the next day—I did not, but a friend of mine did.
JOHN EXLEY . I am a corn-factor, at 75, Mark-lane. I did not purchase fifteen shares of the Reading, Reigate, and Guildford Railway Company of
the prisoner, but I received twelve shares of him on 12th Aug., and three on the 19th—he gave me this memorandum on 12th Aug.—the body of it is my writing, the signature is his—he acknowledges the receipt of 20 l., and authorizes me to sell the shares within three weeks, if the money was not retained—on 19th Aug. he gave me this memorandum, acknowledging the receipt of 30 l., and authorizing me to sell the shares if the money was not returned—I had bought the goods and fixtures at 3, Pancras-lane, for about 18 l.—I bought them of the landlord's brother, who had seized for rent—there was no stock of wine amongst the goods—I took a bill of sale of the broker, and left the prisoner in possession—the prisoner was insolvent in Feb., 1848—the goods which were upon the premises in Aug. were those for which I had taken a bill of sale in the previous Feb.—I removed them in Aug.—the landlord wished me to have them away—he wanted to have the house—the bill of sale I think was dated about 18th Feb.—he gave me the shares on the 12th and 19th Aug.—I gave him 20 l. on the 12th, and 30 l. on the 19th, making 50 l. together, and he was a debtor to me before.
Cross-examined. Q. What does he owe you? A. About 180 l.—I could not take these shares without advancing him money, because he would not give them up—he asked me for a loan on these, and agreed to allow 5 l. when the shares were sold, to go off the old account—he gave me permission to sell the shares if in three weeks he did not redeem them—I am willing to give up the shares now, if the advance is paid me—Mr. Walker brought, an action against me in Oct.
MR. BODKIN. Q. I believe you offered to give up your shares without the money? A. I did at the Mansion-house.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are you willing to do so now? A. No—it was offered by the defendant to give me a Judge's order for the amount—he was willing to do it at that time—that was on 12th March.
JOSEPH MANN . I am a clerk. I became acquainted with the prisoner in June, 1847—I was in his service a short time—I remember his insolvency in 1848—I was a creditor of his to the amount of about 100 l.—he was cleared by the Insolvent Court in March—there was no dividend paid—I have seen this circular about these wines—there was the same thing when I was with him—he was then in Pancrass-lane—he had no stock at that time—I occasionally went to the docks to taste wines—I was with him five months, and left him on 23d Nov., 1847.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you clerk to Mr. Stannard, or master, which? A. I was clerk—I saw an advertisement in the paper, which I suppose was his—I paid 100 l. for integrity when I went in his service in June, 1847—I did not sketch this circular, not a line of it—I recommended a printer to him to print them—I knew it was a circular—I had seen it on his table, and knew the purport of it—I knew it was quite false—I did not take it to be printed—I procured him a printer.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What salary were you to have? A. To commence at 70 l. a year, and to be raised to 100 l.
SAMUEL JARMAN . I am clerk to Mr. Humphreys—I produce these documents from the Insolvent Court—they are certified copies under the seal of the Court in the matter of Edward Stannard—one is the balance-sheet—the amount of debt is 1510 l., and the other is the adjudication—on 17th March an order was made to discharge him from prison.
MR. BALLANTINE to WILLIAM WALKER. Q. I suppose you are aware that these shares cannot be made any use of till they are transferred by yourself? A. I am quite aware of that, not legally they cannot—I am deprived of the
power to dispose of them—I cannot get new ones delivered to me—I receive the dividends still, and I have paid a call on them since.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Eighteen Months.
[trialAccount t18490409-1006]
1006. JANE MATTHEWS , stealing 200 sovereigns, 400 half-sovereigns, 400 crowns, 400 half-crowns, 400 shillings, and 400 sixpences; the moneys of Henry Luard and others, in the dwelling-house of Mary Ann Griffiths.
MESSRS. BODKIN and HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD HUMPHREYS . I am messenger to the London and County Bank in Lombard-street. On Monday, 12th March, I made up a parcel containing 200 l. in gold, and 200 l. in silver—Augustus Prime took the parcel—this is it.
AUGUSTUS PRIME . I am porter to the London and County Bank. On 12th March I took this parcel from the Bank to the Cross Keys, St. John-street—I delivered it to Jonathan Seymour, the coachman of the Bedford-coach.
JONATHAN SEYMOUR . I am the coachman of the Luton coach, which starts at five o'clock in the evening, from the Cross Keys, in St. John-street—I received this parcel from Prime on 12th March, about a quarter of an honr before the coach started—I put the parcel on a desk in the inner bar—Mrs. Griffith was in the outer bar—the prisoner was in the inner bar, sitting by the side of the fireplace—I left the parcel on the desk—I always put it there.
MARY ANN GRIFFITHS . I keep the Cross Keys, in St. John-street, Clerkenwell—it is my dwelling-house, and is in the parish of St. Sepulchre—the Luton coach goes from my house about five o'clock. On the 12th March I saw the coachman come with the parcel into the bar-room—I saw him put it on the desk, on the left-hand as you go into the room—the prisoner was in the bar at that time, sitting opposite the door, a little beyond the fire-place—I think I had spoken to her, but I am not quite certain—I was there when she first came, about twenty minutes before five o'clock—she had a shawl and bonnet on—she asked Miss Bishop some question, and went away—she returned, and after she had returned I saw her sitting in the bar-room when the coachman took the parcel to the desk—the door by which you enter from the bar to the bar-room is a glazed door—I remember a gentleman coming in in a very few minutes after the parcel came—I knew him by sight, he had been sometimes at the house—I did not know his name—I do not think he had anything in his hand—he went into the bar-room—I was inside the bar, and met him coming, before he got to the door which goes into the bar-room—I interposed to prevent his going into that room—he pushed rudely, by me, saying there was a lady there belonging to him—he then went by the glazed door into the bar-room, and went up to where the prisoner was sitting—there was a conversation between them—I heard nothing more than the word "wife," which was said by the prisoner—I was then standing near the desk on which the parcel was—I noticed that it was in its proper state, and done up in brown paper, sealed and directed—this is it—while I was standing near the desk, the gentleman called for a glass of sherry—I went towards the door, and asked Miss Bishop to bring it him—she brought it, and I gave it him—I was all the time remaining in the room—he gave me a shilling to pay for it, and I called to Miss Bishop to give 6 d. change—in doing that I turned from the prisoner and the gentleman towards the glazed door, and I observed, reflected by the glass, the red seals of a parcel being passed by the gentleman across to the prisoner where she was sitting—the gentleman was then between the desk and the chair the prisoner was sitting on—I went to the desk, and there found a different parcel to the one that had been there—this is it—it is
sealed, but has no direction on it—I examined it so as to see that it was not the parcel that had been there before—I turned to the gentleman, and said, "You have changed the banker's parcel!"—I had the sham parcel then in my hand—on my saying that he took hold of my arm, and attempted to pot me out of the bar—I threw this parcel to Miss Bishop, and said, "Get assistance;" but he succeeded in pushing past me and leaving the house—I turned, and saw the parcel drop from the right side of the prisoner—I did not observe where it came from—she had then left the chair and approached the desk—she said, "What is all this about?"—she did not attempt to leave the room then—she had before approached the door, and I said to her, "If he has escaped, you shall not"—a gentleman named Elwin came to ray assistance, and she was detained—there was no other parcel on the desk but the banker's parcel—I had known the gentleman by sight—he had called to leave a coat, and sometimes had a glass of sherry when he fetched it away In the afternoon—there were two men in the house, whom I had noticed before when that gentleman was there—they were standing at the bar window—I believe they had some brandy-and-water—I did not serve them—I did not see anything more of them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Ton only saw the parcel on the ground? A. I saw it sliding down by the side of the prisoner's dress—I am sure that is correct—I was very much flurried—when the man came in, he went up at once and spoke to the prisoner—her shawl had fallen from her shoulder, as I supposed, with the weight of the parcel.
ANN BISHOP . I assist Mrs. Griffiths in the business. On 12th March the prisoner came there from half-past four o'clock to twenty minutes before five—she asked what time the coach went, and asked to be allowed to sit down—she moved towards the inner bar—I told her that room was engaged, she could go into the coffee-room or up stairs—she said there were gentlemen in the coffee-room, and she expected a friend, she would walk out a little way—she went out, and came back—I then asked her to walk into the barparlour, and she did, and sat on a chair near the fire—I saw a gentleman come in—he said, "You have a lady here belonging to me"—that was said loud enough for the prisoner to hear—he pushed rudely by Mrs. Griffiths, and pushed into the room—I heard Mrs. Griffiths ask for a glass of sherry, which I gave her—she then gave me a shilling to give her sixpence change—the gentleman then pushed her, she threw the parcel to me, and he made his escape—she called for assistance, and Mr. Elwin came in—I had seen the gentleman several times before—he used to come in the morning, and ask me to take his coat—he never treated me with anything to drink—I saw two other men in the house, and on one occasion I had seen one of those men and the gentleman walking together in Old-street-road—those two men were at the house that day, and had brandy-and-water—when this took place, they said, "What is the matter?" and went away, leaving their brandy-and-water partly untouched, and not paid for.
REV. THOMAS HENEY ELWIN . I am a clergyman of East Barnet—I was at the Cross Keys waiting for the Luton coach—in consequence of some alarm I went to the bar, and saw Mrs. Griffiths—the prisoner was in the bar-room, and Mrs. Griffiths asked me to assist in detaining her, which I did—the policeman came, and she was taken into custody—she said, "Why am I detained?" and said they were in error.
ELIZABETH LICKFIELD . I keep a coffee-house, in Bridge-street, Southwark—I know the prisoner—she came to me in Feb.—she gave the name of Mrs. Matthews—she lodged three weeks with me.
WILLIAM M'KEWAN . I am accountant at the London and County Bank—Mr. Henry Luard is the general manager of the bank—he is a shareholder and there are others.
JOHN ARCHER ( policeman, G 217). The prisoner was given into my custody for stealing the banker's parcel—she said, "Allow me to have a glass of sherry, or sherry and water"—I then told her she must come with me to this station—she said she was waiting for a Miss Brown, who was going by the coach to Barnet—I said we would wait till the coach started to see if she came we waited, and she did not—I then took the prisoner—I was present where she was taken before the Magistrate—she was asked her name, and she said we might give her any name we pleased, one name was as good as another—on the last examination she gave the name of Jane Matthews.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18490409-1007]
1007. PATRICK CONNER , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Alfred Staniford, with intent to steal.
MR. PAYNE, JUN., conducted the Prosecution.
ALFRED STANIFORD . I am a publican, in George-street, Lisson-grove, St. Marylebone. On 1st April, at a quarter past three o'clock in the morning, I was awoke by my wife, who said she heard footsteps in the room—she jumped up, and I did the same—I looked all round, and saw the prisoner under the bed—I took hold of him and put him on the bed—I sent for a policeman, and he was taken into custody—he said, "I hope you will forgive me this time"—I said, "No, not if you were my own brother"—I knew him to be a hardworking man a year and a half ago.
JOHN MANNING ( police-sergeant, D 44). I was called, and took the prisoner—he had his shoes off—I searched about the yard, and found a ladder against the washhouse—I went up the ladder, and found his shoes on the top of the washhouse—I asked him where his shoes were—he said he had lost them—when he came down he said they were on the tiles.
Prisoner's Defence. Having been keeping company with the servant, I went to the house for the purpose of seeing her, as I was going into the country the next day; and not wishing to disturb her master, and knowing that the window was left unfastened, I thought I would avail myself of it to get in to see her.
ALFRED STANIFORD re-examined. I have no servant—my wife has a sister, but she never had a word with the prisoner—my window was shut at night, and it was open in the morning, when I found him.
GUILTY .— Confined Nine Months.
THIRD COURT.— Tuesday, April 17 th, 1849.
PRESENT —Mr. Ald. WILLIAM HUNTER; Mr. Ald. CARDEN; and EDWARD
BULLOCK, Esq.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq., and the Fifth Jury.
