1010. JOHN COUPLAND and ELIZA COUPLAND , unlawfully assaulting and beating John William Coupland, aged five years, and neglecting to give him sufficient food. ( See page 617.)
MESSRS. BODKIN, CLERK, and PLATT, conducted the Prosecution.
JANE SALTER . I am the wife of Henry Salter; in January, 1848, we lived at 7, Palace-row, New-road; the prisoners came to lodge there at the latter end of March, or beginning of April, 1846—the little girl was born there in the following October or November; a boy named John William came home; he was then about two years and a half old—he was a healthy nice child then; I have heard the parents very cruelly use it; the last time was when I was at the foot of the stairs, and heard the father violently beating it; the mother said something, and he said, "D—n its little eyes, I will beat its b—d belly in"—I heard it screaming, and heard him say, "I will knock its b----y brains out;" he then appeared to dash it against the wainscot—the mother was in the room—the child cried very much, and about ten minutes afterwards it came down stairs for a halfpe'th of milk; it was in a very bad state of crying, and seemed very trembling; he scarcely knew how to walk down stairs—he pulled up his clothes, and said, "Look here, Mrs. Salter, what my father has been doing." I looked at his legs, and from the bottom of the loins to the calf of the leg he was so emaciated, that the blood was raised to the surface of the skin; it was bruises—there were many other such occasions before that, the mother was always very cruel to it from the time it came home; scarcely a day passed but what it was bruised—it was in a very thin delicate state when it left in April, 1848; it was bruised from head to foot—I have given it food in consequence of the cruelty of its mother; the father was urged to beat it by the mother—the language used to be very shocking on both sides—the father used to be away for a month together; but for the last three months, scarcely a day passed without my hearing one of them beating the child.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. When did you leave your house? A. In the latter end of last April; the last occurrence I speak of was a week before they left, the father beat it then—I know that, by the violence of his hands—I did not go to prevent its being murdered, or send for an officer when I saw the marks on its person, I only complained to my lodgers and neighbours—I have talked to the mother on good terms and on bad terms—I do not think I ever saw the child free from bruises for the year and a half it was there—once it had a black eye, and bruises on the forehead at different times—I gave the prisoners notice to quit, as I was going to leave my house—they left the day before me—I do not owe half a year's rent—I gave the prisoner's warning several times, but they would not go—I have not been to any loan society—I believe Mrs. Coupland was sent, but it is a long time ago—my husband and Mr. Coupland were going to have the loan between them—I did not send any forged receipts to the society, and did not forge any—I think the security was not sufficient—I never went to tea with the prisoners—I went into the room when she was confined, and dressed her child—the boy was healthy and stout when he came, and he was healthy when he left, but very weak and emaciated.
MR. CLERK. Q. When you reasoned with her about her conduct to the child, what answer did she make? A. The answer generally used to be, "D—him I hate him, he is so much like his father's friends"—my husband has been paying the rent since we left—it is not all paid.
ELIZA REEVES . I am the wife of William Reeves, a shoemaker of
Somers-street, Hatton-garden—I knew the deceased child—it died about the 27th of January—I lived in the first-floor front—I have heard the male prisoner call the child a d----d young b—r and say he would knock its b----y guts out—I saw the child when it came down to the water-closet; it seemed so weak it could scarcely walk—it was usually kept locked in the front-room—I have seen the aunt throw bread in at the window when the parents were out—at the latter part of the time they altered to the back-room—I have heard a fall in the front-room when no one was there but the child and the prisoners, and have heard a scream immediately afterwards; it appeared in good health, but for the last two months it sunk rapidly—in November last, I heard a noise in the back kitchen like the child being choked; I went and saw the female prisoner polling it out of a tub of water—I had heard the noise three or four minutes before—about three mornings afterwards she put it in again, holding it by the feet with its head downwards; she kept it in about half a minute; it seemed almost suffocated—it was half a butter tub of cold water; she was scolding it, and said it had dirtied itself—I did not speak to her—I was afraid to interfere with her—the man was not there.
Cross-examined. Q. When was this? A. About three weeks before Christmas—I was never in their room—I never had a child—I have got my third husband.
WILLIAM REEVES . I am the husband of the last witness—we lived in Somers-street six or seven months before the child died—its name was John William Coupland—it was nearly five years old—I used frequently to see it—I occupy the front parlour, and it was generally kept in the room over my head, and generally alone—the mother used at times to run up; she would leave it for hours at a time; she used the girl as an angel compared with this child—the boy appeared wasting away for six months—the girl was not so by any means—Mrs. Parker, the aunt, who lives opposite, has frequently thrown bread up to the child—I have frequently heard the child screaming, and heard the father say he would stamp his guts out, and then I have heard the child being beat, and thrown down—that would happen frequently—I was not away from home from morning to night
Cross-examined. Q. You have never had any children? A. Not by this wife, but by my former wife—I am the rather of nineteen, but only two are living.
MART CAVANNAGH . I am the wife of Edward Cavannagh. Last Jan. we lived in the parlour at 8, Somers-street—we were there two months—the prisoners lived in the room above us—the child died there—I have seen it come down—it was in a very bad state, much declined—about five weeks before the inquest I noticed that its face was black from the forehead to the chin—I went to ask it a question, but the mother put her head out at the window, and it did not answer me—I have heard the mother lock it in, and go out without him, and when she has come in I have heard a noise as if the child was beaten, and knocked against the floor—I asked her what was the matter with him—she said he was perfectly well, but it was his own bad temper and dirt left him in the state he was in—the other child was very healthy, and was treated very well, as far as I could see.
Cross-examined. Q. You were never in the room? A. No, only when the child was burnt.
JAMES WRIGHT . I am a surgeon, and lived at Wardour-street, Clerkenwell, at the time of the accident. I saw the child on 26th Jan., in a state of insensibility, and in a very reduced, emaciated state, apparently for want of food—he called for bread and butter, and ate it most voraciously, so that it
quite astonished me—the bread and butter was in the room—I asked the prisoners a few questions, and they told me they had been in a state of great destitution for upwards of twelve months—I said the child must have nourishment—they said they had not the means of getting it—I said if they came to my house they should have a small piece of mutton—the mother went with me for it—I was called next morning, and found the child burnt.
Cross-examined. Q. They sent for you on that occasion? A. Yes; I found them waiting for me—the mother was very far advanced in pregnancy, and has been delivered in gaol—the whole place presented a scene of destitution, but I have witnessed many worse—they had furniture, and bread and butter—she was very thankful to me for the mutton—I found it on the fire next day—she told me they were in a horrible state of starvation—I offered to attend her—a child having mesenteric disease gets emaciated, and very frequently the lungs get diseased—consumptive people get thin—I did not know the child had disease of the lungs till the post mortem examination, at which I was not present—the mother seemed as intent and anxious as mothers usually are—I have seen the girl in the street—she was very healthy—I know that consumptive people eat voraciously.
THOMAS JACKMAN . I am house-surgeon to St. Bartholomew's Hospital. The child was brought there on 27th Jan.—I made a post mortem examination—the cause of death was a burn—the body was in a very emaciated state; the lungs were affected with inflammation of the lower lobes to a considerable extent, otherwise the child was healthy; there were some recent bruises on the head and legs—there was a bruise on both eyebrows, which could not be produced by a child falling on the floor—there were about three bruises on each leg, corresponding with each other, as if the two legs were struck at the tame time—there was no disease about the child to account for the emaciated state in which I found it.
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you been admitted as a surgeon? A. About twelve months—I pay for my place at the hospital, I have no salary—I cannot always tell by inspection what disease has or has not existed.
MR. PRENDERGAST called
MRS. PARKER. I am the female prisoner's aunt, and have known the child from its birth—it has always been a very ailing child, and was in the country with its grandmother more than a year—it returned to town, and I saw it almost every day till its death—I lived right opposite, and have done so twenty-one years—I never saw the father or mother illtreat it in my life—I never saw any marks on his person—the parents have been in a great state of want, but they treated him as well as their circumstances would admit—they have been in a state of starvation—I have known them to breakfast on 2 d. a morning for the last two months: half an ounce of coffee, half an ounce of sugar, and a pennyworth of bread, and I have given them firing to do it with—I have known them to be from Monday to Wednesday without fire or food, she and her two children, when he has been out butchering with Mr. Bamford, but he has had nothing to do since Feb. or March last—I recollect the child being taken to the hospital; the mother carried it—it never complained of its mother's treatment; it wanted to go home to her at the hospital—I once threw a bit of bread in at the window to it, and many times I have thrown to it a bit of sweet-stuff, or gingerbread, or an apple when I have seen it at the window, and he has come down to the door and I have given him something for himself, and his sister too; both when the mother was at home and when she was out; but she never was out much, only when she was nursing her sister—she was an attentive kind mother—she has been
with me in my confinement, and with my husband when he was ill, and three of my children she brought up, and I never knew a bad thing of her in my life—she was a servant before her marriage, and was seven years in one street, and respected and beloved by every one who knew her—if she saw a poor creature in the street she would give them something.
Cross-examined by MR. CLERK. Q. How was it that you threw the things up at the window? A. Because I saw him at the window—the room-door used not to be locked until the latter part of the time, when they thought a broker would be put in for rent, and then Mrs. Coupland used to go backwards and forwards to the child—sometimes she left the girl with me in the morning; the boy was locked up by himself at times, not for five hours together, it might be an hour or an hour and a half—I produce a quantity of tickets of things which they have pledged—the father could get nothing to do—he did not supply Mr. Bamford with meat, sometimes not one day in the week, because she would ask me to lend her money to get Mr. Bamford some meat—I have advised her to apply for relief, and a lady living by, gave her a Philanthropic ticket, and I went to a lady in Hatton-garden to get coals and bread—I know Mrs. Coningsbee; I do not recollect her saying something to which I replied that I had thrown the child up some bread and a turnip to quench his thirst, but that if she interfered the mother would scratch her eyes out, she was such a wretch—I never threw a turnip into the room—I saw the child at the hospital, but did not see any bruises on it.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did the mother go to the hospital? A. Yes; and she would have gone again at night, but I prevented her, as her pregnancy would not admit of it, but she went next morning at half-past five; I called her up.
SARAH ANN DAY . I am the female prisoner's sister, and live with my mother in Cambridgeshire. The child was with her some time—it was wry ill, it scarcely enjoyed a week's health during the eighteen months it was there—it never was strong—the mother's conduct to it was very kind indeed, and the father's also—I have visited them in town since the child came up, it still continued unhealthy.
Cross-examined by MR. CLERK. Q. Were you living with your grand-mother? A. Most of the time; I was in service six or seven months—I believe I saw the child the day before it left its grandmother; it was very unhealthy then—my mother had a doctor's bill of 16 s. to pay when it left—I was not at Mrs. Salter's while they lived there—I have not been in Somers-street above once.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you see the child while you were in service? A. Yes; it came to me several times, and I always found it unhealthy.
LYDIA ASHTON . I am the sister of the female prisoner, and live at 2, Red Lion-passage, Holborn, a quarter of a mile from Somers-street, I frequently saw the child—ray sister was always very kind to it, and her husband also—it was always very delicate and weak—the mother's conduct at the hospital was very kind and attentive—I have given them money several times to support them, and produce duplicates of their pawned property.
Cross-examined. Q. How often have you been to the house? A. Two or three times a week, before and since Christmas—I saw no bruises on the child at the hospital.
SARAH TICKTUM . I attended the woman in her confinement, at the birth of the child in question, until her mother came from the country—I always found her most affectionate towards it—I have known her eleven or twelve years—the child was always very pale, sickly and delicate from its birth—I
was only there as a friend, not as a nurse—I have not been bribed to come here—the man has always been kind and affectionate to her and the children.
Cross-examined. Q. Where do you live? A. 15, Rathbone place, Oxford-street—I have known her ten or eleven years—I have not seen her so much lately as formerly—I did not visit them at Somers-street or at Mrs. Suiters, the mother has called on me and brought the child with her, but not lately; it was more than six months ago—she behaved very kindly to it,
MR. HODSON. I am a licensed-victualler, of Great Warner-street, Clerkenwell. I knew the female prisoner before she was married—she lived with Dr. Coombe, of Caroline-street—I have known her up to this time, ten or eleven years—she has always borne the character of a kind, affectionate, well-disposed woman—she has been in the habit of calling on me the whole of that time, more or less—I have lived in her neighbourhood for the last two years, and have seen her once or twice a week.
Cross-examined. Q. Where used you to see her? A. At my residence in Great Warner-street.
JOHN COUPLAND. Aged 29. GUILTY of an Assault.
ELIZA COUPLAND. Aged 35, Confined Six Months.

1011. JOHN EATON and CAROLINE EATON , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Purslow, and stealing therein 86 yards of cloth, 2 coats, and 16 waistcoat-pieces, value 28 l. 16 s., his goods. MR. COOPER conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH PURSLOW . I am the wife of John Purslow, a tailor, of 6, Upper King-street, Bloomsbury. On the night of 22d Feb., I fastened up the shop—I saw the shop-door last at eight o'clock next morning—about twenty-five minutes past eight my daughter called me—I found the shop open and everything taken from the counter.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had not you opened the shop door that morning? A. No, I found it open about ten minutes after I came down—the shutters were not taken down—my husband was up-stairs—he is not here—the property has not been traced.
SARAH PURSLOW . I am the daughter of the last witness. On 23d Feb., about half-past eight, I opened the passage-door and saw the female prisoner—site asked if my father Wanted a waistcoat-maker—I went up to ask him, came down, and told her "No"—she said, "Thank you," and shut the door—she looked very stout, and part of her gown was spread over the handle of the door—in about five minutes I found the door wide open, and called my mother down.
Cross-examined. Q. When you saw a stout woman at Marylebone, did you say you knew her by her bonnet and breast? A. Yes; I had a good view of her face, and am quite sure she is the party.
WILLIAM HARRIES . I am a sawyer. On 23d Feb., about half-past eight, I was taking down my shutters, and saw the male prisoner coming out of Mr. Purslow's shop with a bundle of clothes, and give it to a woman under an archway—I could see two rolls of cloth, and part of a coat—I followed them as far as Orange-street.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen the man before? A. No, but I cannot be mistaken in him—I did not tell Mr. Purslow—I thought they were parties who worked for him—I did not notice his eye—the right side of his lace was to me—( prisoner's left eye was injured).
MICIAIH REED ( policeman, E 108). On 16th March, Miss Purslow pointed out the female to me in Great Marylebone-street—I told her the
charge; she said she was innocent, that she lived at 49, Lowman's-hill, Westminster—I found there was no such number—I afterwards went to 30, Tottenham-street, and found this piece of iron, which she said she used to roast her meat with—it is used as a pick-lock—( The prisoner Caroline being taken in labour, Mr. Cooper consented to a verdict of NOT GUILTY with respect to her.)
JOHN EATON — GUILTY .

1012. JOHN EATON was again indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Henry Eld, and stealing 54 pairs of boots, value 25 l., and 1 pair of shoes, value 3 s.; the goods of James Towers,
WILLIAM HENRY ELD . I am an appraiser, of 28, Denmark-street, in the parish of St. Giles's-in-the-Fields. I let the shop to Mr. Towers—on 8th March, between half-past ten and eleven at night, I went to bed—the house was fast; you cannot get into the shop without coming through my private door
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You were not the last person up? A. The lodgers might be up.
GEORGE GODDEN ( policeman, F 128). On 8th March, about half-past twelve at night. I found Mr. Eld's door ajar; it was fast when I went my round about twelve—I went into the passage and called the landlord up—as he came down stairs the prisoner and two others rushed out of the shop—I caught hold of the prisoner and another—they all three struck me on the head and dragged me into the street—the prisoner fell down; he got up, and ran away—I never lost sight of him—I took him to the station—the other two escaped.
Cross-examined. Q. Where is the shop-door? A. At the corner of the street, but it shuts up with shutters, no one can get in there; the other door is two or three yards in the passage—I took the prisoner about a hundred yards from the house, and am sure of him—he turned a corner, but I did not lose sight of him—he was stopped—I was not six yards behind him.
JOHN HOBBS FROST ( policeman, F 23). I heard a cry of "Stop thief!"—I saw the prisoner coming as hard as he could—I stopped him—he said that was all he got for doing police duty.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was Godden? A. Six or seven yards behind.
JOSEPH BARFIELD . I lodged with Mr. Eld. On 9th of March, between twelve and one o'clock in the morning my dog barked; I went down and found a policeman in the passage—I said, "What do you want?" and before he could answer, three men rushed out—the prisoner was the third; he struggled with Godden—they both fell in the passage, and again in the street—the prisoner ran, and Godden after him—I picked up a hat where the prisoner fell, and took it to the station—he said, "That is my hat, give it to me"—I returned to the house and found a bag full of boots with the mouth to the ground, with another bag laid over it, and a pile of boots beside it, a key and padlock lay on the desk.
Cross-examined. Q. Does not the door open toward the stairs? A. Yes; but I could see—I had past it before they came out—the prisoner struck me in the face.
JOHN GIBBS ( policeman, F 158). On this morning I went to Mr. Eld. I saw a bag of boots and shoes in the shop, and another empty on it—I produce this padlock and two keys; one key was in the door, it must have fitted by a person acquainted with the house.
WILLIAM GUMMER . I am shopman to James Towers; I left the back shop-door fastened with a padlock and common lock—the front door was fastened—I
do not know either of these keys—the boots and shoes were all in their places when I left—the prisoner had come to the shop before that with a female, and bought a pair of ladies boots—a man was outside the shop, who appeared to be waiting for them—the prisoner asked to go backwards—he was gone about a second—the padlock was not in the door then, but the staple was—he passed the door.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not Mr. Towers say, any stranger could make a key to fit the padlock, and he suspected you? A. Yes; I was at home that night, at 14, Stacey-street—I take the keys with me—I bring the boots in from the door, and put them on the floor at night.
MR. COOPER. Q. You had no hand in making these keys? A. No, I never saw them before.
GUILTY .* Aged 32.— Transported for Ten Years.
OLD COURT.— Wednesday, April 18 th, 1849.
PRESENT —Mr. Ald. GIBBS and Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant and the First Jury.

1014. WILLIAM JONES and JOHN DUNCAN , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Mary Morrisson, at Islington, with intent to steal. MR. EWART conducted the Prosecution.
MARY ANN MORRISSON . I am the daughter of Mrs. Mary Morrisson, of 39, Suffolk-street, Lower-road, Islington. On 30th of March, I and my mother went on a visit, and shut the house up; we went to it on the Friday following, 3d of April, and found the kitchen window broken, the shutters forced in, and the sash down.
WILLIAM JOHN HALL . I am an engraver of 14, Suffolk-street. On 3d of April, between one and two in the morning, I saw Mrs. Morrisson's gate open—I went to the back-parlour window, and fancied I heard voices—I went to the door and waited till a policeman passed—he went with me to the back
of the house, and saw the window open, and found the prisoners by the wall—the policeman took them.
Crass-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did not Jones say he was intoxicated? A. Yes; there is a turning by the house—you can get over the wall easy enough.
WILLIAM ACASON ( policeman, 423 N). Hall spoke to me—I went with him into his garden—the window-sash next door was open—the prisoners were standing close by the back kitchen—I took them in charge—Jones said they had had too much to drink—Duncan attempted to pass me—I found this life-preserver on him—I got assistance, and they were both taken.
THOMAS GOODSRHAM ( policeman, N 433). I heard a rattle spring, and went to 13, Suffolk-street, and found the prisoners in Acason's custody—I received Jones—I saw Duncan throw away this jemmy —a knife, and some lucifers were found on Jones—Duncan said, "If they send me to Hobart Town, I can't be worse off for I have done nothing lately, and I meant to do it to-night, but you have spoilt it"—I found five marks on the kitchen door.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you pick up the jemm? A. Inside the railing of No. 14, about twelve yards from where Duncan stood, but he had been within half a yard of that place—it was dark—I saw him -fidgeting something in his band before he threw it—this is not the first time I have said that Duncan used the words, "You have spoilt it;" but I will not be positive as to those words ( the witness's deposition being read, did not contain those words)—I told the Magistrate so.
JOHN BARTRON ( policeman, N 65). I went to the premises—I saw the prisoner Duncan throw away these skeleton keys ( produced)—the kitchen window was broken.
ELIZABETH WBLCH . I am a widow. On 3d of April, about halt-past eight o'clock, I was at No. 18; the kitchen window was safe, and the shutters bolted. MR. HORRY called
ROBERT LEAD . I am a hair-dresser, of 66, Long-lane, West Smithfield; Jones has lodged at my house eight or nine months—I recollect the morning he was taken—the night before, he went out at eleven o'clock, very drunk; he said he would not go to bed, he would try to walk it off—he took out some lucifers to light his pipe—he always carried them, being a very great smoker—I knew him three or four months before he came to me—his character has always been honest—I never knew anything to the contrary.
Cross-examined by MR. EWART. Q. What do you call very drunk.? A. When I went up he was asleep by the fire, with his head hanging over, as if he was going to be sick—I wanted to go to bed, and tried to rouse him—he has been a cellurman at a public-house close by—I never knew him to be out after twelve o'clock at night—it is generally half-past ten or eleven—I have had a son convicted—I have five sons,
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 17.
DUNCAN — GUILTY . Aged 30.
Transported for Ten Years.
( John Robert Foulger, City police-sergeant, 89, and Michael Haydon, City-policeman, 21, stated that both prisoners were the constant associates of thieves, and that Lead's back-door was kept open all night for them; the witness Lead was committed for contempt of Court.)

1019. GEORGE THOMAS BUNKER , stealing 1 sovereign; the moneys of Henry William England.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY WILLIAM ENGLAND . I am a surveyor and auctioneer. On 31st March I was at the Sutton Arms, Caledonian-road, Islington—I supped there with the landlord, and about half-a-dozen select friends—I was leaving to go to my own residence, about half-past eleven—as I fronted the bar, the prisoner stood there with two other persons—I had seen him at my house the day before on business—he said, "What are you going to stand?"—I said, "Anything you like, but it is your turn"—he said, "I have no money"—I said, "Well, have what you like"—I ordered Mrs. Clayton to draw them what they liked—there were three glasses of brandy and water had, one of soda-water, and a cigar—it came to 2 s.—I had one glass of brandy and water—I threw down a sovereign, and said to Mrs. Clayton, "Take for what we have had out of that"—she turned her head to a little parlour to get change, and the prisoner took up the sovereign and put it into his pocket—I said, "Don't be a fool; pull that sovereign out; let me pay for the reckoning"—he said, "I have not got it, I did not take it up, I have no money"—Mrs. Clayton came back, and the prisoner borrowed 2 s. of one of his friends to pay the reckoning—he seemed in a jocular mood—I still insisted on having the sovereign—we left the house, and came on towards my residence—I could not find a policeman—when we got to a coffee-shop, which was open, I said, "You shall go no further, I am determined to have the sovereign"—we went in there, and had some coffee—I sent a friend to fetch the policeman—the prisoner still said he had not got the sovereign, he had not got any money at all—I saw the prisoner searched at the station—the sovereign was found in the pocket which I pointed out.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Where did you meet with the friend that you sent for the policeman? A. At the coffee-house door—he is my own family doctor—the prisoner said he lived in Chichester-place—he keeps a corn-dealer's shop, or something of that sort—he came to me the day before, and wanted a customer for the shop—I think my clerk wrote down the particulars—the supper that night began about half-past eight o'clock—I should say I might have had one glass more than enough—I had had about two glasses of brandy and water—I was sober, but not perfectly sober—I have been charged with felony—I was discharged in that case—I had paid for an article, and it appeared that the party who sold it me had not come by it properly—I tendered the receipt before the Magistrate, and he said that the prosecutor ought to be placed in my position—I charged a woman I had gone home with, with stealing a watch—it was afterwards brought to me by the policeman, and the woman was discharged—the Magistrate asked me whether I wished to press the charge, and said had I not better take the watch back—I did so—the Magistrate dismissed the case, but said he would leave it open for me to indict him—I preferred this bill, not behind the prisoner's back, he knew it perfectly well—I told him at the police that I would indict him—the Magistrate allowed the sovereign to be placed in the policeman's hands—he did not bind me over to prosecute.
CLAYTON. My husband keeps the Sutton Arms. England supped at our house on 31st March—when he was leaving, the prisoner asked him what he would stand—he said anything he liked to drink—they had brandy and water, and one of them had soda-water—Mr. England took a sovereign out of his pocket and told me to take the reckoning—he put it on the counter towards me—I went into the bar-parlour to get change—on my return I did
not find the sovereign—Mr. England said to the prisoner, "Why don't you put the sovereign down; you have got it in your left-hand pocket"—the prisoner said nothing, but borrowed 2 s. of Mr. Nickling, and paid Mr. Clayton for the brandy and water—they seemed to be wrangling about the sovereign, and Mr. Clayton told them it was time to close the house—they then left.
JAMES ABRAHAMS ( policeman, N 296). Mr. England gave the prisoner in my charge—the prisoner said he had no money: I am quite sure of that—when Smith asked for the pay for the tea, Mr. England said, "Who will pay for it?—the prisoner said, "I have got no money"—Mr. England paid for it—when I got within about 200 yards of the station the prisoner said, "I do not disown having a sovereign; I borrowed it"—when I got to the station I found a sovereign in his left-hand trowsers-pocket—Mr. England had said it was there.
Cross-examined. Q. Who fetched you? A. I cannot tell; some gentleman, Mr. England was behind him—I was in Maiden-lane, about 200 yards from the coffee-shop—I do not think the prisoner or the prosecutor were sober—it was about twenty minutes before one o'clock—this case was heard before the Magistrate and dismissed—I heard the Magistrate say to Mr. England, "You can indict him at the Sessions if you like."
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did be direct you to keep the sovereign? A. Yes, till the case was settled.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18490409-1020]
1020. CHARLES THOMAS AUSTIN , stealing 413lbs. weight of lead, value 4 l. 10 s.: the goods of Ebenezer Tonkin.
RICHARD COX . I am in the service of Mr. Glasbury, a butcher, of the Terrace, Kensington. On Friday night, 12th Jan., I saw the prisoner outside the door, about ten o'clock, with five men—I had seen them about a quarter of an hour before—I saw the men potting some lead into a van before Mr. Tonkin's door—it came from under Mr. Tonkin's window—I saw the prisoner lifting up the tail of the lead into the van—another of the men said to the prisoner, "He has treated us very well, shall we have another pot?"—the prisoner said, "No, we won't have any more"—that man then walked off—the other four men then ran away, and the prisoner was left alone with the van—he got up into it and drove the horse off towards London—Fisk went in the same direction.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How near were you to them? A. Two or three yards—the Magistrate desired that the prisoner should be admitted to bail—I did not hear the men desire the prisoner to give them a lift up—the prisoner's name and address was on the van—Mr. Tonkin's house is the next house to the Adam and Eve—there is a court between—there is a large gas-light at the Adam and Eve—this lead was outside Mr. Tonkin's shop—if a man wanted to go from there to Knightsbridge on an improper errand there are plenty of bye-roads to go.
HENRY FISK ( policeman, T 98). I received information from Cox, and overtook the van near Hyde-park-corner—the prisoner was driving—I desired him to stop—he drew up—I found in the van the piece of lead taken from Mr. Tonkin—there was another man in the van, who jumped out and ran away—I took the prisoner—the horse was warm, as if it had been running—the prisoner said he had been hired by a man to go to Kensington to fetch some goods, but he did not go further than the Adam and Eve.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it a clipped horse? A. No—the lead was 3cwt. 2qrs. 21lbs.—it was a good horse—it was about two miles from the place it was taken from.
EBENEZER TONKIN . I am a plumber, of the Terrace, at Kensington. On the night of 12th Jan. I had some lead rolled up under my window—I saw it safe at eight o'clock—I afterwards met the cart and saw the lead—it was mine.
HENRY SAMUEL COOPER. I keep the Adam and Eve. I saw the prisoner about a quarter after nine on the night of the 12th Jan.—there was one other man with him, and several about—they drank together—he was at my house about three quarters of an hour.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner? A. No—I have ascertained that he was a soldier in the Guards, and that he lets out vans.
JOHN LOCK . I know the prisoner—he lets out vans. On the evening of the 12th of Jan. a man named Drake came to a house and inquired about him—the prisoner came, and Drake spoke to him about moving some goods.
NOT GUILTY .

1021. EMILY PHILPOTTS , stealing 1 pair of stockings, value 6 d.; the goods of John Willett.
JOHN HEATH . I am shopman to Mr. Willett, a linen-draper, of Barking. On the 20th March, in the afternoon, the prisoner came and bought several things, and paid me about 5 s., for them—she returned, and desired to look at some black cotton stockings—I showed her some at 6 d., and said we had some at 1/2 d.—she said she would take a pair at 6 d.—I put them into her parcel with some more goods which she bought—after that, and as she was about leaving, I missed a pair of stockings—I told my master—he followed her out of the shop, sent for a constable, and found the stockings on her.
Prisoner. I paid for one pair—you took another pair and rolled them up in my parcel—I said I had one pair, and that was sufficient. Witness. You did not say so—I did not give you the second pair—you must have taken them while I went to inquire the price of a piece of print for you—I had counted the stockings by my master's order before putting them before you—you had been a customer before.
JOHN WILLETT . I saw the prisoner in the shop—I had reason to suspect her, and ordered Heath to count everything he put before her—he told me something—I stopped her going out of the shop, and accused her—she said she was sorry for what she had done, and would never do it again, if I would forgive her—I found the stockings in her apron, and the pair she had bought in the parcel.
Prisoner. I did not say so; I said if I had a pair not belonging to me, I did not know it. Witness. You did not.
JOSEPH MANSER ( policeman, K 36.) I took the prisoner—she gave me this pair of stockings—as I took her to Ilford, she said she bought both pairs—there was another pair in her bundle.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
NOT GUILTY .

1024. WILLIAM WILSON , stealing 1 sack, value 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Edwin Mollett and another.
RICHARD AMOS ( policeman, E 390). On the night of 6th April, about half-past eleven, I was on duty on Chadwell-heath, and saw a wagon going towards London—after it had passed I saw the prisoner come from behind a shed, go to the back of the wagon, and take something off; I do not know what—he crossed the road towards a hedge, and was out of my sight about two minutes—he then crossed the road again and went towards his home—I knew him before, and knew where he lived—just before he got home he threw something over some timber, and went in doors immediately—I went to the place and found this sack ( produced)—I then met Crawford, and we went to the prisoner's house and took him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you not had a quarrel with the prisoner? A. No—he was a little the worse for liquor—I never had a dispute with him, and said I would do for him some day—when I found the sack, the wagon was gone on towards London, and I went after the prisoner—he passed us just at his own door, and wished me good night—this was about eleven miles from London—the prosecutor's is about twenty miles from London.
JOHN DANCEY . I am Mr. Mollett's horse-keeper. On this night I was with the wagon, and had care of it—we were going towards London—there was a sack of potatoes on the tail—it was safe at a quarter-past eleven, when we were about 100 yards the other side of Chadwell-heath—when I got off the wagon at Ilford turnpike I missed it—this produced is it—it is Mr. Mollett's.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it? A. By these particular places, and this hole is where I tied a string round to keep the potatoes in—I cannot say how many sacks of the same sort my master has got—I have been in his service fifteen or, sixteen months.
JAMES CRAWFORD ( policeman, K 253). I met Amos about half-past eleven, and in consequence of what he said I went to Wilson's house and took him into custody—Amos produced this sack, the bottom of which was very dirty, as if it had been in some ditch—the front of the prisoner's smock was all over dirt the same as the sack.
CHARLES MOLLETT . I am in partnership with my brother, Edwin Mollett—this sack is our property.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18490409-1025]
1025. JOSEPH HARRIS , stealing 4 1 l. 2 yards of doeskin, value 20 s.; the goods of Horatio Talbot.
JOSEPH BENTON ( policeman, K 381). On the evening of 1st March, I saw the prisoner and another lad near Mr. Talbot's, the tailor's shop, at Stratford Church—I watched them about the shop for three quarters of an hour—the other lad went into the shop and took something out, crossed the road, and gave it to the prisoner, who was about three yards off when he went into the shop—I ran to them, and tried to take the prisoner—he dropped this cloth as I was grabbing him, and ran away—I kept close to him—a lad stopped him, and I took him.
GEOROB STRBETER . I am in Mr. Talbot's service—he has a shop in
Stratford—in consequence of what Benton said I missed a piece of doeskin; this produced is it—I know it by this shop-mark, which is my own doing—it is Mr. Talbot's property—I had seen it safe not half an hour before, on a piece of board standing up inside the shop-door.
CHARLES MILNER . I live in Dog-row, Mile End—I was coming from Woodford, and just by Stratford Church saw something on the path, picked it up, and it was this piece of cloth—it was three or four hundred yards from Mr. Talbot's shop—I saw a policeman and gave it him.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going down the road, and a boy came across to me with a piece of cloth; he dropped it, and I did not have it at all.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18490409-1026]
1026. MARY GIBBONS , stealing 1 bed, 1 nightgown, 1 scarf, and other articles, value 16 s. 1 d.; the goods of William Vernon, her master.
WILLIAM VERNON . The prisoner was in my service nearly five months, and was going away on 9th April, in consequence of what my wife said to me—I told her to have the boxes searched—I saw this scarf taken from her box in her presence; it is my daughter's, who is ten years old; also a baby's night dress, which is my property—the other things were taken from her box before I got to the room; they are all my property—the prisoner did not say anything.
EMMA VERNON . I am the prosecutor's wife—the prisoner sent for me early in the morning to look at her boxes—when she was going away, and the boxes were corded up, I felt one box, and was certain it was heavier than it ought to be—it was opened in her and Rutherford's presence, and this feather bed and all this property ( produced) found in it.
Prisoner. Q. Is the bed your property? A. Yes; it was what you used to sleep on—she said she was very sorry she had taken it, she had had a little drop to drink.
ANN RUTHERFORD . I am also in Mr. Vernon's service, and was present when the box was searched—I saw the bed found; it is Mr. Vernon's property; and the baby's bed-gown too—I know it by having a mark on it.
Prisoner's Defence. The bed is my own—master's bed is there still
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.— Confined Eight Months.

1027. HENRY SEXTON, feloniously stabbing John McCarthy, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. W. J. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MCCARTHY . I am a labourer of Stratford. On Sunday, 4th March, I met the prisoner, who I had worked with, and said, "Henry, you want shaving;" he said, "Yes, I do, Jack—I said, "Let me shave you," and shoved this broom across his face—he made a kick at me—I rubbed it across his face again; he. said "You b----b----, I will rip your b----guts open"—he put his basket down and pulled a knife out—I ran across the road, and did not know he was behind me till I felt him stab me—I said, "I am stabbed" he said, "Go along"—I put my hand behind me, and showed it him full of blood—I became senseless—Perry took me to a doctor.
JOHN PERRY . I am a boilermaker, and was with M'Carthy—I saw him brush the prisoner's face several times—the prisoner made a kick at him, and threw the basket from his shoulder; took a knife out, ran after him, and stabbed him, then picked up the basket and walked away—I heard him say, "You b----b----, I will rip your guts open if you do that again!"—I said,
"Harry, you have stabbed the man;" he said, "I know I have, and a b----good job too"—the broom did not strike him, it only brushed him.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I give you half my tobacco? A. Yes. SAMUEL WATFORD. I am an engineer—I saw M'Carthy brush the prisoner's face—the prisoner made a kick at him, and M'Carthy brushed him across the face again—the prisoner dropped his basket, took a knife out, opened it, put it by his side, and placed his right hand over the blade; M'Carthy brushed him across the face again and ran away; the prisoner ran after him and stabbed him—I said, "What a villain you must be to stab a man in the back like that;" he said, "He should have let me alone"—I asked a gentleman to hold him, and took the knife from his pocket—there was blood on the blade.
JOSEPH TUCKET ( policeman). I found M'Carthy bleeding dreadfully from a wound in the side; I produce his trowsers, here is a cut in them corresponding with the wound—the prisoner said, "He should not have hit me with the broom."
WILLIAM HENRY HOLMAN . I am a surgeon at the London Hospital—the prosecutor was brought there on 4th March with a wound in the small of the back, an inch wide and an inch and a half deep; it had bled a great deal, it was not dangerous, but it would have been if it had been a little more on one side.
Prisoner. I have had my head cut open, and often do not know what I am doing; my head has been sown up; I have a scar two inches long. Witness. There are marks on his forehead and the top of his head—they are old wounds.
WILLIAM COX ( Thamespoliceman). I have known the prisoner sixteen or eighteen years—he was always quiet and inoffensive—they play all manner of games with him, and push him down—his brother is an idiot.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Six Months.
KENT CASES.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1028. AMBROSE IVES , feloniously assaulting John Chandler, with intent to rob him. MR. PLUMPTREE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN CHANDLER . I am in the service of Mr. Dodenham, a coal and corndealer, of Lewisham. On 26th March, about eight at night, I was walking through Lewisham, carrying a truss of clover; I met the prisoner and a man I do not know; he pushed the clover off my back; I asked the reason, but had hardly got the words out, when the prisoner collared me and pushed me backwards into the ditch, and fell on me, with his hand on my throat—he put his left hand into my pocket while he held me down with his right—I had no money—I had him by the jacket; he asked me to leave go of him, which I did, and he ran and turned up Mr. Dadd's field.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you hit me with a fork, and say, "You b----r, what did you do that for?" A. No; you collared me—I knew you by sight—I am sure you are the man.
WILLIAM WILKINSON ( policeman, R 235). On 26th March, between eight and nine o'clock, Chandler came to the station, and next morning I took the prisoner—I told him it was for assaulting a man and attempting to rob him—going
along he said, "I am the man, I knocked the truss off his shoulder, and we went into the ditch together, I punched his head twice; a little further on the man turned round on me with a fork, and I hit him, and got up and ran away."
Prisoner's Defence. I was the worse for drink, knocked against the hay, and it fell; he said, "You b----r, why did you do that;" I rushed into him, and shoved him into the ditch; he pulled me in with him; I caught hold of him with one hand and hit him with the other; I got up and ran away.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 19.— Confined Six Month.
[trialAccount t18490409-1029]
1029. THOMAS CARTER , stealing 1 pair of boots, value 7 s. 6 d., the goods of Thomas Anthony Hand. MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS ANTHONY HAND . I am a bootmaker, of Woolwich—on 31st March, about five o'clock in the afternoon, I was called, and saw the prisoner in the shop—he said he wanted a pair of Cossack boots—I fitted him with a pair—he said "I will have these; I have given the boy half-a-sovereign, the price is 9 s."—he had not asked the price—the boy looked up astonished, and said, "You have not given me any money"—the price was 7 s. 6 d —he refused to take them off, and I sent for a constable—the boy was searched, and no money found on him, he bears a very good character—these boots are mine ( produced).
CHARLES CARROLL . I am Mr. Hand's apprentice—on 31st March the prisoner came into the shop, and at that instant I called my master—the prisoner never said a word before he came—the first pair of boots fitted him—he said "I gave the boy half-a-sovereign"—I said, "You did not give me any money, sir"—the prisoner said, "The boy says the price of them is 9 s."—I had not said so; the price was 7 s. 6 d.—he had given me no money.
JOHN FORSTER . I am a sergeant in the Royal Artillery—the prisoner belongs to that company—he was under stoppages, and has only received fourteenpence since June last—he was not likely to have half-a-sovereign.
THOMAS WHITEAR ( policeman, R 258). I took the prisoner—he had a pair of new boots on, which he would not take off, but insisted on keeping them—I think he was sober—I searched Carroll, but found nothing on him.
Prisoner's Defence. The boy said he would get me a pair, and asked 9 s. for them; I laid half-a-sovereign on the counter; when I said so, the boy denied it. GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Four Months.

1030. GEORGE LARKIN , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Waite, and stealing 2 pairs of boots, 1 loaf, 8 ozs. of butter, and 1 cake, value 9 s., his goods.
SARAH SARTAINE . I am servant to James Waite, of Acton-row, Woolwich—on Sunday morning, 18th March, I came down about eight o'clock and found the kitchen and wash-house doors open—I was the first person down—persons could get from the yard into the wash-house, and from thence into the kitchen—I missed some butter and some boots from a cupboard which was not locked, and some lucifers from the mantel-piece—I had seen the things safe at ten o'clock the night before.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Are they not making a railway there? A. Yes, there are a great many navvies about.
ELIZABETH VEALE . I am Mr. Waite's mother-in-law—on 17th March I went to bed shortly after twelve o'clock—I was the last person up, and locked all the doors—the door between the kitchen and wash-house was
locked and bolted—I put a pair of boots on the safe in the cupboard—the butter was safe then—when I came down I missed a loaf and some cake, two pairs of boots and a towel.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was the towel? A. The cake was wrapped in it.
CAROLINE CAHILL . I am the wife of John Cahill, of 53, High-street, Woolwich, and keep a clothes-shop—on the Monday night, between nine and ten o'clock, the prisoner came to my shop with this pair of men's boots—he said they were his own—I gave him 2 s. 3 d. for them—I gave them to the constable—Chapman afterwards brought this other pair.
MART CHAPMAN . I am single—on 19th March, about eleven o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner at the Marquis of Granby—he asked me to go home with him, and gave me this pair of women's boots ( produced) as he said he was short of money—I took them to Mr. Cahill's.
Cross-examined. Q. You are an unfortunate girl? A. Yes.
ELIZABETH VEALE re-examined. These boots are my daughter's, and these others my son-in-law's.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Twelve Months.

1032. ANN CHICK , stealing 1 shawl, 2 watches, 5 yards of satin, and other articles, value 20 l.; the goods of Alexander Davidson.
CAROLINE DAVIDSON . I am the wife of Alexander Davidson. In March, 1848, I lived at 3, Harding-street, Woolwich; it is now pulled down—the prisoner was a lodger of mine—on 18th, March she assisted me in cleaning the yard—I went into my room, and she said, "Oh! look at your chest of drawers and your boxes"—I said, "Oh! lam robbed!" and ran to the door to see if I could see any one—she said, "Sea if your watches are gone"—I looked and missed them, and a jacket, three pairs of trowsers, eleven waistcoats and shirts—I found a shirt in the passage, and two silver watches, one of which had hung over the mantel-piece, the other over the drawers, about three-quarters of an hour before—I also missed a satin dress, and a crape and cashmere shawl.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Was she married? A. Yes—she had lived at my house six months with her husband—she gave me warning that day—a policeman had been to inquire into the matter—they left before their week was up—her husband had gone out at half-past eight o'clock—he said he had nothing to do with taking the things—I saw them all safe after he left—I believe her husband left her in May, when he heard of the robbery—she has had a child since.
ELIZABETH CLARK . I am the wife of Charles Clark; the prisoner married my brother. I went to his house, 12, Kidd-street, Woolwich, in May, and found a duplicate of a shawl, among a variety of others which he gave me—he insisted on having the box opened, and she tore up a great many.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you go with one of them to remove it? A. Yes; at the latter end of January this year—she had left my brother on the last day of May—a child was born on 3d June; it died at about nine weeks old, and he was summoned to pay the funeral expenses, and paid them—since she has been sent for trial, Mrs. Gale, her mistress, has sent for him—I do not know that he went to get her wages—he has not contributed to her defence to-day—the bonnet I have on does not belong to her—I bought it in Woolwich—the
ribbon did belong to her—I have got two old bonnets of hers, which were left when she went away.
ALEXANDER DAVIDSON . This watch, trowsers, and handkerchief are mine and were taken from my dwelling-house.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A boiler-maker. I was present when a bundle, containing a satin gown-piece, a watch, waistcoat, and other articles, was found in the cesspool, when the house was pulled down.
JOSHUA THORPE . I am a pawnbroker. I produce these things pawned on 20th March, 1848, by a female, in the name of "Mary Taylor"—this shawl was pledged in June, and renewed last January, in the name of "Ann Chick;" here is the duplicate—the prisoner has frequently pledged in the name of "M'Donald."
Cross-examined. Q. How lately? A. Not for nine or ten months—I do not think I have seen her since May 31st—some of the tickets have been renewed since, by Clark, and other persons.
CHARLES MAY ( policeman, R 296.) I took the prisoner, and told her the charge—she said she had taken the shawl, but did not know anything about the other things—her mother came to the station, and said to her, "If you know anything about Mrs. Davidson's robbery tell me, and I will endeavour to redeem them for her"—she said, "You can't redeem them all; there is a coat and trowsers at Mr. Moore's; and when I saw the policeman coming towards the house, I ran and put the things down the cesspool"—they most have been there about twelve months.
Cross-examined. Q. You found her living in Mr. Dale's service? A. Yet; he is a doctor, at Commercial Road East—they said she had lived there some time—her mother is respectable. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 25. Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Judgment deferred.

1036. WILLIAM KNIGHT , and JOHN JONES , unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin. MESSRS. BODKIN and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution,
SARAH MORRIS . I keep a tobacconist's shop in Bear-lane, Greenwich. On 13th March a young man in his shirt-sleeves called, and asked for an ounce of the best tobacco and a halfpenny pipe, and paid roe with a crown-piece—I gave him the change, and he went away—I put the crown into a bowl with some small silver, but there was no other crown—a policeman came in directly—I marked it, and gave it to him.
JOHN WHITE , ( policeman, R 180). On the evening of 13th March I was in Deptford Broadway, in plain clothes—I saw the two prisoners, and watched them for an hour—they went to the other side of Greenwich College, and at last they came to Mrs. Morris's—Knight went in there in his shirt-sleeves—Jones remained opposite the shop—he then went and placed himself by a post about thirty yards off, in sight of the shop—when Knight came out he went in the direction where Jones was—I went into the shop, and received from Mrs. Morris this crown—I then went in pursuit of the prisoners—I came up with them in three or four minutes—Knight then had a jacket on—I took the prisoners into custody—I saw Jones put his hand into his pocket and drop something like a piece of rag into an area—I said they were charged with passing a bad crown at Mrs. Morris's—they said they knew nothing about it—I searched Knight, and found on him some tobacco in a paper, a half-crown, and two shillings—I pointed out to Serjeant Carpenter the area where I had noticed Jones to drop something.
THOMAS WEBB . I am a porter at Greenwich Hospital. On 13th March, about half-past seven o'clock, White pointed out Knight to me in Mrs. Morris's shop—Knight came out of the shop, and I assisted the officer in taking the two prisoners—I saw Jones throw a bag or a piece of rag down an area—I showed Carpenter where it was, and saw him pick it up—it had a crown-piece in it.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-sergeant, R 38). I was on duty with White—I saw the two prisoners in company—they went through several streets—I did not see Knight go into Morris's shop, but I saw Jones standing a little way off in a slanting direction from the shop—he had Knight's jacket on his arm—I saw Knight come out of the shop, and run across the road to Jones—they went into a corner, and Knight took his jacket and put it on—I followed them, but lost sight of them—I saw them again about twenty yards off, and White had got up to them—an area was pointed out to me where I found this. little bag, containing this 5 s.-piece wrapped in this paper.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint These crowns are both counterfeit, and cast in the same mould.
Knighfs Defence. I know nothing about the counterfeit crown; this other man is a total stranger to me.
KNIGHT — GUILTY . Aged 21.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 12.
Confined One Year.

1037. ISABELLA ELLIOTT and JANE ELLIOTT , stealing 1 coat and other articles, value 2 l. 6 s. 6 d.; the goods of Daniel M'Sweeney: to which JANE ELLIOTT pleaded GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Month.
DANIEL M'SWBENET . I am a Greenwich pensioner, and live at Deptford. On 7th March I lost my kit, which was ail I had in the world, except what I stood up in—they were all in my chest in my own bedroom—I know the
prisoners well; they are mother and daughter—they were living next door to me some time ago, but they left and came back to the same street again—the articles that are here are mine.
SARAH KEN FELL . I am the wife of William Kenfell, and live in High-street, Poplar. On 8th March, Jane Elliott came to my house—she said, "Here are some things which belonged to my father; he is dead, and they are of no use to my brother, because they are old-fashioned"—I bought these trowsers, and shirt, and waistcoat of her, for half-a-crown—when the officer came I gave them up to him—these trowsers are very old-fashioned; he owns himself that they were made three years before King William died—this shirt I valued at 6 d.—this waistcoat has a piece put in the back of it—she had the coat with her, but she told me she was not going to sell it, but to take it on board ship to her brother.
WILLIAIT LOVELL . I am a dealer. Jane Elliott came to me, and offered to sell the coat for 1 s. 6 d.—I said I would not buy it of her at all—she then said her mother would come, and she did come, but I would not have anything to do with it—the mother said her husband gave it her to do what they could with it—it is a uniform coat, with the buttons cut off—I would not buy it.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman, R 180). I took the prisoners, and told them they were charged with stealing a jacket and other articles from M'Sweeney—they said they knew nothing about it—I asked the daughter where she had put the jacket that she offered for sale to the marine-store dealer—she said she gave it to the man she had it from—the mother said, "You had better tell the truth"—she then said it was up-stairs—I found it, and found the buttons that came off the coat—I took the daughter to the station—she then told me the other clothes were at Poplar—the mother's husband is a sailor, and is at sea—she has two or three other small children.
Isabella Elliott's Defence. I came home, and my daughter told me she had the coat given her; she said she had sold the other things.
ISABELLA ELLIOTT — NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

1039. WILLIAM LEWIS and FREDERICK ALLEN , stealing 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 s. 9 d.; the goods of James Francis; Allen having been before convicted.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman, R 180). On 7th March I was in the Broadway, at Deptford, between three and four o'clock—I saw, the two prisoners in company with another boy, who is not in custody—I watched them for about half an hour, over to Church-street, Greenwich, to Mr. Francis's shop—I saw the prisoners loiter about there for half an hour—I then saw Allen take a pair of trowsers, and give them to Lewis—I took the prisoners—Lewis threw the trowsers down, and said he had not got them.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-sergeant, R 38). I was with White—I saw the prisoners—I watched them some time, but finding they knew me I got out of the way, got to the back of the shop, and ran round—I saw Lewis bringing this bundle under his arm—he turned back, ran away, and threw it into the gutter; it was this pair of trowsers, with a ticket of 6 s. 9 d. on them; they were inside the shop.
Lewis. I threw them down in the road, not in the gutter. JAMES FRANCIS. These are my property.
GEORGE LANGDON ( policeman, N 265). I produce a certificate of Allen's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted July, 1848, confined three months)—he is the person.

1040. WILLIAM PAGE and ISAAC BULL , stealing half a pig's head, value 1 s.; the goods of John Bayley; and that they had been before convicted.
WILLIAM TUCK . I live at Mr. Farebrother's, a baker, ill Wellington-street, Woolwich. On the evening of 29th March I was going home—I saw the prisoners on the opposite side of the road—I saw Bull take half a pig's head from a tub at the door of Mr. John Bayley, a cheesemonger and grocer, and run away with it—Page ran away also—they were together when Bull took it.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman, R 122). I went to a lodging-house, in High-street, Woolwich—I found the prisoners—I found a cloth on Bull, in which some damp salt meat had evidently been wrapped—I told him he was charged with stealing half a pig's head—they both denied it—I did not find the half head; that was impossible, after they got it into the lodging-house—there were forty or fifty persons in the lodging-house.
Page. Never a One of the half heads in the barrel weighs more than four pounds, and they were marked 3 1/2 d. a pound: the shop is a nuisance to the street.
THOMAS ROBERT EVEREST . I am a constable, of Northfleet, in Kent. I produce a certificate of Bull's former conviction, from Mr. Wilde's office, at Maidstone—( read—Convicted Jan., 1849, on two indictments, confined six weeks, and whipped)—the prisoner is the person.
WILLIAM GLADWIN re-examined. I produce a certificate of Page's former conviction—( read—Convicted April, 1847, confined one month)—he is the person.

1041. JOHN DAVIES , feloniously stabbing Ellen Davies in the back, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Ptosecution.
EMMA SHAW . I am single; I live in the house in which the prisoner lodged, at No. 19, Artillery-place, Woolwich. He is a soldier in the Artillery—he is married to a woman named Ellen—on Monday, 12th March, they had a quarrel, and she used very harsh language, not fit to repeat—he struck her in the face with his hand—she did nothing, but continued calling him names, and he hit her again; I believe in the face—she still continued to call him names—they were then quiet a short time, and she began again calling him names—his bayonet was close before him on the table, in its sheath, and he drew it out, and stabbed her with it in her back—her back was turned towards him—he put the bayonet back into its case again—he did not think he had hurt her—he had been packing his clothes before, and I believe he still continued packing them—she fell down, and said "Oh, my God, he has stabbed me!"—I sent for a doctor, who came and attended to her in about ten minutes or a quarter of an hour—the prisoner did not render her any assistance—she was lying on the bed—she bled very little—I did not undress her, I waited for the doctor—she did not cry any more—the prisoner was not in the room
above a quarter of an hour—when he had packed his clothes, he took them away, leaving his wife wounded.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Had you known his wife long? A. No; only two or three months, by seeing her—I know she was very much given to drink, and he intended to separate from her; he was going to barracks—she had promised several times not to drink—it was not to prevent his going away that this quarrel arose—I had seen her that morning before the prisoner came in—she had been in liquor all the morning—this happened between eleven and twelve o'clock, she was drunk before eleven—I took a knife out of the woman's hand when she was drunk—she had been eating her dinner—I took it from her because I was afraid she would strike the prisoner, as he had struck her—I did not see her raise the knife at him, or show any disposition to use it—we were all eating our dinner, the prisoner and I, and Mrs. Davies, and a little girl—the prisoner was eating dinner during the quarrel—it had begun before dinner—the prisoner had been cleaning his bayonet, and put it in the case—when he struck her she was standing leaning against the fireplace—the quarrelling had ceased for a little while, and then she began again calling him names, and calling his mother very offensive names—he went on packing, and appearing not to think that he had done any harm—when she fell down, he said she would not fall to hurt herself, and he took no notice—there was no blood on the bayonet—it was done in the heat of passion.
MR. HUDDLESON. Q. Was the prisoner drunk? A. No—the quarrel began before dinner, and he struck her in the face—after that they sat down to dinner—while they were at dinner they began quarrelling again, and he struck her again; then she got up with the knife in her hand, and I took it out of her hand—after that we were all sitting down to dinner—he gave her a slap of the face, she fell off the chair, and then she got up and stood by the fireplace, and then he struck her with the bayonet.
ELLEN DAVIES . I am the prisoner's wife. I was in liquor on that day—I was very angry—I do not recollect what took place.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe your husband had been obliged to separate from you about two months before? A. Yes—I recollect going to his captain when he had been beating me—I was in the habit of getting drunk sometimes—I wish to throw my husband on your protection—I do not wish to give any evidence to hurt him.
GEORGE SHIPMAN . I am a surgeon at Woolwich. I was called to attend Ellen Davies—I found a wound between the third and fourth bones of the back—I probed it, it was from an inch and a half to two inches deep—it had just escaped the kidneys—it was a part from which I should not expect much blood—the locality of it prevented its bleeding, there are no large vessels there—it was a triangular wound, which is a wound of a dangerous character—this bayonet would produce such a wound—it had entered the flesh—it was perfectly straight—it had gone by the side of the kidney, where it is soft and fat—it might penetrate some distance without making a wound that would be dangerous to life—if it had been a little lower down it might have been dangerous—I visited her three times a day till the Friday following, and twice afterwards—I attended her from the 12th to the 21st of March—if it had been a round wound, I should not have had half the trouble I had—a triangular wound never heals kindly, when two lips of the wound are closed the other remains open—I was afraid of an inflammation—it was between the spine and the kidney.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman R 330). I went to the barracks and got this
bayonet ( produced)—I took the prisoner into custody—he said he had received great provocation.
EMMA SHAW re-examined. This is the sort of bayonet that he struck hit wife with—I undressed her—the clothes were cut, but very Hule—it was just below her stays, it went through her gown and two white petticoats—It had passed through her linen.
Prisoner's Defence. I came off guard, and found her drunk on the bed; I determined to pack up my knapsack and go to the barracks, which the captain would prove; he knows that she came to him before, and promised to behave better if I would go back to her.
(The prisoner received a good character from his captain and several other persons).
GUILTY of a Common Assault. Aged 23.— Confined One Month.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

1042. WILLIAM LEONARD and LAWRENCE RYAN , stealing 1 rope fender and 6lbs. weight of rope, value 1 s.; the goods of James Thift, in a vessel in the Thames. MR. SIMONDS conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE FINLATSON . I am the mate of the Guildford, which was lying in the Mill-hole, Rotherhithe. On 30th March, we had a rope fender safe over the ship's side—I missed it when the police-inspector came—Mr. James Thift is captain and part owner of the ship—I have since seen the fender at the police-court—it is Mr. Thift's property.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Is the master's name on the stern of the vessel? A. No; it is on the boats—the crew consists of six men forward, and me and the captain—I do not know whether the men had been backwards and forwards to the shore.
WILLIAM THOMAS BRIDGES ( Thamet-police-inspector). I was on duty on 30th March, between twelve and one o'clock at night, and saw a boat fast to a barge between the Guildford and Enterprize —I went alongside with two constables—Elwick and Howe jumped out of our boat into the barge—Howe brought the prisoner Ryan to me, and found the fender in the barge—I said to Ryan, "How about this rope in the boat?"—he said a man sent him to the barge to get them—I said "What man?"—he said he did not know—Elwick then brought Leonard to me, and I asked him if the boat belonged to him—he said, "Yes"—I said, "What about this rope?"—he said a man had sent him who he did not know to get it, a traveller along-shore—this fender ( produced) is one lost from the Guildford —I did not see it fonnd.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you first see either of them? A. They were brought to me by the constables in the barge where the fender was found.
WILLIAM ELLWICK ( Thames policeman, 27). I was on duty—I remember Bridges coming on board the barge—I brought the prisoner Leonard to him—I found this rope in the head of the barge—I saw Leonard with his hand upon another rope which hung down from the side of the Guildford —I afterwards examined that, and found the piece I found Leonard with had been undone from that.
Cross-examined. Q. You found the rope belonging to the Guildford in the barge? A. Yes; the prisoner's boat was fastened to the barge—I heard them say a traveller told them to get it—the prisoners work for the Trinity Corporation, and this boat belonged to the Corporation—they had been
drinking, but were not drunk—they were sober enough to have charge of a boat.(The prisoners both received good characters.)
LEONARD — GUILTY . Aged 38.
RYAN — GUILTY . Aged 37.
Confined Nine Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoners.)

1044. WILLIAM HARRIMAN and CHARLES GREY, unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin. MR. CLERK conducted the Prosecution
ELIZABETH BIFFIN . On the 9th March the prisoners came to my beer-shop in the Waterloo-road—Harritnan asked for a pint of fourpenny ale, and gave me Is.—I kept it in my hand, and gave him 10 d. change—he kept talking to me and drew my attention—'Grey called for a pint of ale and gave me 1 s.—I sent a boy for change, who returned and told me it was bad—I then looked at the first and said, "This is a bad one"—he said, "If that is a bad one, I hare been getting it at skittles"—Harriman said, "Let me look at it," took it out of my hand and put it into his mouth and said, "See I can swallow it, and I can swallow a bushel of them: give me a glass of ale to wash it down"—I saw no more of it—Grey got nothing from me.
Harriman. You asked me whether I gave you. 6 d. or 1 s? Witness. I did not.
SAMUEL BEAGLEY . I received 1 s. from Mrs. Biffin to get change—I took it to Mr. New, the butcher, who bent it and gave it me back, and I took it to Mrs. Biffin—the prisoners were there.
HENRY BURR . I was at the beer-shop and saw the prisoners—Mrs. Biffin gave me a bad shilling, I put it to my mouth and almost bent it double, and gave it her back—I afterwards saw Harriman swallow it—he asked for a drop of beer to rinse it down
WILLIAM ROMA IN ( policeman, L 88). I went to the beer-shop—the prisoners were given into my charge—Harriman was very violent, and said he would not be taken—I said he was accused of swallowing a bad shilling—he said yes he had, he could swallow a bushel—Grey said he would be b—d if he would go, he had no bad money about him—Mrs. Biffin said, "If you search him, you will find it on him"—I took his hand from his trowsers-pocket and he said, "Oh! you can search me"—I searched him and found this shilling ( produced) between the fingers of his left hand.
Grey. I have two fingers off my left hand—you took the shilling out of my pocket. Witness. I did not; the first joint of his fingers only are off—I did not prompt Mrs. Biffin when the depositions were making out, and say I must tutor her a little, as she was a stranger.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . This shilling is counterfeit
Harriman 's Defence. I had not had 1 s. that day.
Grey's Defence. I had only come home that afternoon from Corfu; I borrowed the money of my sister.
Grey called '
MATILDA SMITH . I am a widow; Grey met me accidentally and borrowed half-a-crown of me.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Grey a relation of yours? A. My brother—I live
in Oakley-street—he comes there at times—I cannot say when I saw him last—I do not know where he lives when he is at home—he wag rather tipsy that day—he has been to sea since 1847, but I do nut know the name of the ship, or where his last voyage was to.
HARRIMAN — GUILTY . Aged 38.)
GREY — GUILTY . Aged 37.
Confined One Year.

1045. ELIZABETH SMITH and EMMA MARTIN were indicted for a like offence.
JAMES CHAPMAN . On 3d March the prisoners came to my tavern at Rotherhithe,"and asked for half-a-quartern of gin—Smith gave me 6 d., it was a little worn, I put it into a bowl in the till—there was a new sixpence there of the Queen's reign—a policeman came—I took it out, found it was bad, and marked it.
THOMAS TOWERSEY ( policeman). I saw the prisoners go into Mr. Chapman's—I went in after they came out, and received sixpence from him; I came out, and saw the prisoners in front of the Black Bull—I took Martin, and told her the charge—she said she knew nothing of it
ANN CARPENTER . I keep the Black Bull, at Rotherhithe. On 3d March the prisoners came, Martin asked for half a quartern of gin, and gave me 6 d.—I tried it, and said it was bad—she appeared surprised, and asked Smith If she could give me 2 d., which she did—I put the sixpence into a bowl in the till—there was nothing there but farthings—a constable came, and I gave it to him.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( policeman, M 89). I took Smith, and found on her 1 s. 10 1/2 d. and a fourpenny bit —I produce sixpence, which I received from Mrs. Carpenter.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . These two sixpences are counterfeit, and from the same mould.
